Report of the Senior Grand Warden
Per Capita Recommendation for 2010-2011
William J. Bray III, Senior Grand Warden

As your Senior Grand Warden, I am responsible for preparing and
presenting to the Grand Lodge a proposal for the per capita assessment that will
be collected in 2010 to fund the Grand Lodge fiscal year for the period November
1, 2010 through October 31, 2011.
Beginning with my term as Junior Grand Warden, I have worked with
the Executive Committee, the Finance Committee and the Grand Lodge staff to
develop projections of the income and expenses necessary during the 2011 fiscal
year to operate the Grand Lodge, fund our strategic priorities and initiatives, and
to provide programs and services to our lodges and members. The Executive
Committee is committed to the strategic plan approved by the Grand Lodge in
2005 and we work diligently to make progress in all the areas of concern and
strategic importance.
The proposal that I enclose for you seeks to maintain the programs and
services of our Grand Lodge while planning for certain efficiencies that will allow
us to overcome the economic challenges that face all organizations today.
In order to establish an appropriate per-capita assessment, I have prepared
the attached budget. You will notice that the total expenses are approximately
$6,000 less than the proposed 2009/2010 budget prepared by our Deputy Grand
Master. While we have managed to keep expenses flat, there are certain factors
that will increase the per capita.
•

Membership loss. We project a total loss of 2,342 members, which
represents a loss in revenue of $72,602. This factor taken alone, we
would need to increase per capita $1.29 to make up for the loss in
members.

•

Impaired Investments. As every other organization, our investments
have decreased in value. Because of our distribution policy, however,
there will only be a small reduction in 2010/2011, but it does impact per
capita by seven cents.

•

Reduced Nob Hill Dividend. The parking garage business has been
affected by the downturn in the economy just as almost every other
industry has. We are projecting less than normal revenues from the Nob
Hill Masonic Center and this alone would require an increase of per
capita by $1.36 to make up the difference.

•

Inflation will represent increased expenses of just under $33,000 or
$0.58 in per capita.

92

Proceedings of the

With these factors alone, we will need to increase per capita by $3.30.
There are a number of factors, however, that will relieve this necessary increase.
First, in the area of general and administrative expenses, we have made
efforts to contain costs without cutting services or programs.
•

Wages. Potential salary increases have been reduced to 2% from 3.5%
in the current year.

•

Travel has been reduced by 10% for the 2010 fiscal year and has been
frozen at those levels for 2011.

•

G&A expenses have been held to a 2% or 2.5% increase.

These measures represent about a $32,000 savings, which offsets the
inflation we discussed earlier. In addition to these reductions, expenses have been
cut in the fraternal and appropriations categories where possible.
•

Fraternal spending has been reduced by just over $25,000 representing
a savings of $0.48 in per capita.

•

Appropriations have been reduced by about $13,000 saving an additional
$0.24 in per capita.

The result of these combined reductions is the need for a $2.00 increase
in per capita to offset the factors that have decreased our revenues. In summary,
the 2009/2010 per capita was $31.00. The loss in revenues and inflation would
increase per capita by $3.30 for the 2010/2011 fiscal year. Through cost cutting
measures, we are able to offset those increases by $1.30 in per capita. The
necessary per capita for the 2010/2011 fiscal year is $33.00. There is a graph
that follows the budget that illustrates the revenue sources, explains the per capita
calculation, and provides a three-year per capita comparison.
Freemasonry in California has great forward momentum. The economy
is challenging all organizations, including our Grand Lodge and Lodges, but
through careful planning we can remain committed to our progress and be sound
stewards of our financial resources. I ask for your support as we work together to
sustain and grown our honorable fraternity.

There are several factors that affect per capita, membership levels and inflation being the
major factors. The membership analysis for the 2010/2011 fiscal year projects a total loss in
membership of 2,342 members. While this is one of the lowest loss of members in decades,
the membership decline will result in lost revenue of $72,602 or $1.29 in additional per capita.
Overall inflation is projected to be under 2.0%. This will result in additional expenses of just
under $33,000 or $0.58 in additional per capita. Due to economic and market conditions, the
reduction in Endowment distribution and Nob Hill Masonic Center dividend will result in
revenue loss of just over $80,000 or $1.43 in additional per capita. All other things being equal,
the per capita would have to be increased by $3.30 to $34.30 in order to make up for lost
members, revenue reduction and inflation. Because of offsets from expense reduction efforts
in fraternal, appropriations, travel and wages expenses, the proposed per capita will only
increase by $2.00 to $33.00.

Report of the Grand Treasurer
To the Most Worshipful Grand Lodge, F.& A.M. of California:
The manner in which your Grand Lodge has dealt with this past year of
financial difficulties represents the epitome of the old adage, “When the going
gets tough, the tough get going.”
Each of you is aware of the state of the general economy and the
investment markets over the last year. While those conditions affect the Grand
Lodge’s financial activities, just as they do everyone else, the Grand Lodge has
survived them well, through the heard work and diligent efforts of our efficient
staff, and the Grand Lodge Officers, Board members and Committeemen.
Budgets have been tightened and closely monitored. Efforts to conserve and
curtail expenses have been effectively applied by everyone involved.
Your Grand Lodge Investment Committee has intensified their customary
diligence over the Grand Lodge’s investment portfolio. They have, over the last
year, made some studied changes in the make-up and allocation of the portfolio
to better reflect economic and investment conditions going forward. In addition,
they have recommended a reduction in the percentage of distribution to be taken
from the portfolio for current budgeting purposes from the traditional 5% per year
to a 4.5% level, a move that has been adopted by the Grand Lodge and each of the
other entities participating in that investment pool.
Your Grand Lodge Insurance Committee has secured significant
reductions in the premiums paid for the necessary levels in both property and
liability coverage for all our entities, including the Grand Lodge itself. Those
premium reductions have helped considerably in reducing our overall expenses.
The ability of the Grand Lodge and other entities to weather the financial
storm as they have is a testimony to the disciplined and conservative manner
in which they have conducted their financial affairs for many years, particularly
in the good times. It has allowed the various entities to continue providing
uninterrupted programs and services to their constituents, without budget-induced
lay-offs of staff and with balanced budgets all the while.
You can be very proud of the work that has been done by all those
involved in the financial affairs of our Fraternity, as I am, and you should offer
them your thanks.
The detailed information I am going to give you today is for a ninemonth period from the start of this current fiscal year, November 1, 2008 through
July 31, 2009. I will compare that information to like data for the same period a
year earlier. This report is already posted on the Member Center website under
Forms/Annual Communication. It will continue to be available to you there until

98

Proceedings of the

the official proceedings of this Communication are published. Those Proceedings
will contain the customary audited financials for the entire fiscal year.
It should be noted that this partial-year information has been prepared
internally by our fine financial staff and has not been audited.
As of July 31, 2009, your Grand Lodge had assets of $5,614,848 and
liabilities of $1,751,895 for Total Net Assets of $3,862,953. Those assets were
some 11/7% lower than at the same date a year earlier, largely due to the decline
in investment values. Keep in mind that most of the assets you might attach to the
California Grand Lodge are not actually owned by the grand Lodge itself and do
not appear in these numbers. I am referring to major assets such as this facility,
which is owned by the California Masonic Memorial Temple Corporation and
our homes in Union City and Covina, which are owned by the Masonic Homes of
California, both separate corporations.
The 12 months ending July 31, 2009 have been difficult ones in the
investment markets. The investment accounts of the Grand Lodge are down by
12.4% during that 12-month period. The annualized return for the prior three year
period was -1.6%, and +3.5% annualized for the prior five-year period.
Your Grand Lodge continues to operate in the black on a year-to-year
basis. For the nine-months ending July 31, 2009, the total income as $1,977,834,
while total expenses were $1,561,732. That income is some 14% higher than the
comparative period last year. Expenses for the nine-month period ending July
31, 2009 were slightly higher than the same period last year, a difference that is
largely due to the timing of various expenses. While the Net Excess as of July
31, 2009 is some $416,102, it should be noted that approximately 20% of the
total year’s expenses occur in the last three months of the fiscal year. The current
projection is that the Grand Lodge will end the fiscal year on October 31, with a
slight surplus.
I continue to be most impressed with the concern and diligence which
your Executive Committee and the financial staff bring to the monitoring of the
actual income and expenses versus the budget. The numbers are compiled and
studied by all parties in great detail on a monthly basis, as the year progresses.
You should know that any surplus at the end of the fiscal year is not carried over
into the next year, but rather it is permanently transferred to the Endowment Fund.
The result is that each Grand Master starts his year with a zero balance, with only
the budget for that year to work with.
The nine-month expenses were divided in the following manner: 23%
for Fraternal matters (such as this Annual Communication, travel expenses of
the Elected and Appointed Grand Lodge Officers and Ritual Committee and the
insurance for the constituent lodges), 27% for Appropriations and Programs (such
as various lodge support Committees, the Freemason magazine, and Committees
such as Policy, Jurisprudence, Investment and Trial Review), and 49% for General

Grand Lodge F. & A.M. of California

99

and Administrative items (such as salaries and benefits, accounting and auditing,
investment management fees, legal fees and general office operations).
I must offer one word of caution about the future. Many of our budgeting
processes use projected investment withdrawal amounts that are the result of
averaging investment values for the prior five years. That policy has served us
very well in that it softens the severe decline we have experienced in the last
two years, by including some of the previous positive years. If the investment
returns in the next several years do not equal the years we will be eliminating
when calculating the moving average, the recent declines will continue to have a
dampening effect on our available investment withdrawals. In other words, we
are not out of the woods yet in our outlook.
I again pay tribute to the many highly dedicated brothers who serve on
the various Boards and Committees of this Grand Lodge and its related entities for
the extraordinary amount of time, energy, dedication and hard work they devote
to the business and fraternal affairs of our California Masonic community. Each
of you should seek them out and give them a hearty “Thank You” for the work
they do.
The dedication, efficiency and hard work of the Grand Lodge staff deserve
special comment. Under the leadership of our Grand Secretary, Very worshipful
Allan Casalou, this group of talented men and women perform exceptional service
for your Grand Lodge and its members, all of us, with little, certainly not enough,
recognition and appreciation. Our Grand Lodge organization and administration
is the model among sister Grand Lodges and it due largely to our Grand Secretary
and his staff. Please join me in thanking them.
Allan and I have developed a personal rapport and a working relationship
that has been a pleasure to me and, I believe, a benefit to the Grand Lodge. For all
that he does, I thank him personally.
And finally, my brethren, I thank each of you for having allowed me
to work with you and for you during this past year. I am extremely proud and
honored to be a part of the California Masonic Grand Lodge team.
Respectfully submitted,
Glenn D. Woody, Grand Treasurer

103

Report of the Grand Secretary
Most Worshipful Grand Master and members of the Grand Lodge,
I am pleased to present this, my second report, to you as your Grand
Secretary.
Lodge and Membership Information
As of today there are 340 chartered lodges in our jurisdiction, including
two lodges Under Dispensation and four research lodges. We began the Grand
Lodge year with 340 lodges and two were consolidated. Grand Master Adamson
issued two dispensations for new lodges.
Our membership at the beginning of the fiscal year was 63,497. We had
2,149 gains, approximately 150 more than the previous year; and 4,005 losses,
approximately 200 less than the previous year. The ending membership on June
30, 2009 was 61,044. While we still recorded a net loss of 1,846 members, this is
the smallest loss of members in this jurisdiction since 1967. A more detailed report
of membership activity, together with a number of Lodge by-law amendments,
and veteran’s awards is included in my written report to be distributed later.
Corporate Services
The Grand Secretary serves as the corporate secretary to the boards that
work together with the Grand Lodge to carry out our overall mission. These boards
include the Acacia Creek Communities, the California Masonic Foundation, the
California Masonic Memorial Temple, the Masonic Homes of California, and the
Nob Hill Masonic Center, Inc. The Grand Secretary’s office provides centralized
administration, finance and accounting, legal counsel, human resources, fund
raising, communications, and information technology services to these entities.
Today a cabinet of talented and dedicated professionals assist the Grand
Secretary in carrying out these responsibilities and delivering first rate business
services to the Grand Lodge and its related entities. I wish to express my gratitude
to this incredible team:
Philanthropy
Our Code directs the Grand Secretary to oversee the collection of
contributions to the Masonic Homes, the California Masonic Foundation and our
other charitable organizations. To that end, the Grand Secretary maintains an
Office of Philanthropy, led by Brother Doug Ismail, Chief Philanthropy Officer.

104

Proceedings of the

Despite tough economic times, our brothers dug deep to support Masonic causes
this year, knowing their support would assist those who need help the most. I am
pleased to report that more than six million dollars was received in donations this
year. Nearly $1.6 million was received through the annual fund drive alone, which
is the highest amount raised through the annual fund in any year in our history.
You can read more detail about our fund raising efforts and charitable giving
programs in the 2009 Annual Report to Members, published and distributed at this
Annual Communication.
Because of these difficult economic times, the number of brothers who
needed assistance grew significantly this year. I am proud of how our fraternity
responded to their needs. In partnership with the Masonic Homes of California
we have stretched forth a helping hand to these brothers and their families. As
part of our efforts, we created one centralized contact for all areas of relief in our
state. We call this program simply: “Masonic Assistance.” A toll-free number has
been established that is staffed by those trained to respond to all types of need and
to refer our worthy brothers, their wives, and widows to the appropriate Masonic
relief program. This new toll-free number is printed on the backside of the newly
designed dues cards for 2010. The Masonic Homes has a booth this weekend
about this program if you are interested in more information.
Program Services
The Grand Secretary’s office provides support to the many programs
of the Grand Lodge in the area of communications, membership development,
leadership development, and Masonic education and formation.
In the area of communications, we are proud that our public website
at www.freemason.org was selected by the Society of National Association
Publications as the best member website among more than 750 membership
organizations in the United States; awarding our site the gold award in general
excellence. For the first time in the 56 year history of the California Freemasons,
we published six issues of the magazine this year. And, we launched our electronic
leadership newsletter, The Leader, which was distributed monthly to all of the
lodge and Grand Lodge leaders.
Over 1,000 people participated in our leadership training programs this
year, which included an Inspectors Seminar, four Wardens Leadership Retreats,
two Secretaries Retreats, five Lodge Management Certification Programs, and five
Masonic Formation Programs. In addition, we created four new online training
programs. We now offer six courses online for our lodge members and leaders.

Grand Lodge F. & A.M. of California

105

Recognition of Foreign Grand Lodges
It is my responsibility as the Grand Secretary to keep the Grand Master
informed on matters pertaining to the recognition of foreign Grand Lodges and
to present to you in the form of a motion the recognition of certain Grand Lodges
which meet our criteria therefore. Recognition is the granting of authority
for Masons in this jurisdiction to have Masonic Communication, which is
communication involving the esoteric or secret portion of the Ritual, with Masons
of a Lodge chartered by or holding allegiance to the recognized Grand Lodge.
In practice, this generally means Masons who belong to Lodges chartered by
recognized Grand Lodges may visit each other’s tiled meetings. The terms of
recognition may also include the privilege of dual membership. Our Grand Lodge
may recognize only Grand Lodges that:
A.

Are regularly formed by subordinate Lodges which trace their
origin to regular and legitimate Ancient Craft Masonry;

B.

Hold undisputed sway as the acknowledged sole or concurrent
supreme power in Ancient Craft Masonry in the territory in
which they claim jurisdiction;

C.

Confine their authority and the exercise thereof to the three
degrees of Craft or Symbolic Masonry; and

D.

Subscribe to the General Regulations of Masonry.

We are aided in the evaluation of Grand Lodges seeking recognition by
the Commission on Information for Recognition, a committee of the Conference
of Grand Masters of Masons of North America. We are honored in California that
our very own Past Grand Master, Most Worshipful M. William Holsinger, is a
member of that important Commission.
Prince Hall Grand Lodges
It is the practice of this Grand Lodge to extend recognition to Prince
Hall Grand Lodges that meet our criteria for recognition and where concurrent
jurisdiction has been established by the Grand Lodge we also recognize in the
same jurisdiction. To date, we have extended recognition to 31 Prince Hall Grand
Lodges in the United States with lodges chartered in 39 states, the District of
Columbia, Guam and Turkey.
As Grand Secretary, I try to stay apprised of the recognition activity of
our sister Grand Lodges across the country and find that there is one additional
Prince Hall Grand Lodge that meets the above criteria for recognition. Our
recognition is made on the same basis that recognition was previously exchanged
with the Grand Lodge sharing the same jurisdiction.

106

Proceedings of the
The Most Worshipful Prince Hall Grand Lodge
Free and Accepted Masons of Delaware
The Most Worshipful Prince Hall Grand Lodge Free and
Accepted Masons of Delaware was consecrated in 1849. The
Grand Lodge of Delaware A.F.& A.M. entered into a compact
of recognition with this Prince Hall Grand Lodge on August
12, 2006.

Most Worshipful, I move that the Grand Lodge of California extend an
offer of mutual recognition, on the same basis that recognition was previously
exchanged with the Grand Lodge sharing the same jurisdiction to the Most
Worshipful Prince Hall Grand Lodge Free and Accepted Masons of Delaware.
Motion ADOPTED.
Dispensations and Charters
As I mentioned earlier, Grand Master Adamson issued dispensations to
two new lodges in our jurisdiction this year.
Prometheus Lodge in San Francisco was issued a dispensation and was
instituted on December 16, 2008. Paideia Lodge in Santa Cruz was issued a
dispensation and was instituted on September 3, 2009.
In accordance with Section 801.065 of the California Masonic Code,
Prometheus Lodge U.D. has petitioned this Grand Lodge for a Charter. In
reviewing the records of the Lodge and the information from the District
Inspector and Assistant Grand Lecturer, it has been determined that Prometheus
Lodge has met the requirements of the petition. And having done so, it is the
recommendation of the Grand Master that the petition be granted and that a
Charter be issued. Accordingly, Most Worshipful, I move that a Charter be issued
to Prometheus Lodge U.D. and that it be entered into the Registry of this Grand
Lodge as Prometheus Lodge No. 851. Motion ADOPTED.
Appreciation
As I conclude my report, I wish to extend my appreciation and gratitude
to the 340 lodge Secretaries and 178 Assistant Secretaries in this jurisdiction
whose dedicated work is the backbone of our lodges and our Grand Lodge.
I would also like to thank the District Inspectors and Assistant Grand
Lecturers who work so diligently with the Grand Secretary’s office on numerous
administrative matters throughout the year. Because I work so closely with them,
I see first hand the work of our Assistant Grand Lecturers and I am inspired by
their devotion to our craft.

Grand Lodge F. & A.M. of California

107

I also work closely with the many boards and committees of the Grand
Lodge. In total, there were over 190 board and committee meetings during the
year. While I cannot take the time now to share with you the great work of all
of these leaders, allow me to take a moment to express my personal thanks to
Most Worshipful R. Stephen Doan, consultant for the Grand Lodge Committee on
Jurisprudence. Most Worshipful Doan has provided a great deal of assistance to
the Grand Secretary’s office that, without any doubt, goes above and beyond what
anyone could expect from a brother; and for his assistance, I am very grateful.
I have been given the great fortune to work with our Grand Treasurer,
the Very Worshipful Glenn Woody. He is an extraordinary colleague and a great
friend and I thank him for all of the truly excellent work he has done on behalf of
our fraternity.
I am blessed to work with an incredible staff of men and women at the
Grand Lodge Office, including our offices in Covina and Union City, whose
service to our Grand Lodge is fueled by the good work that each of you do every
day in your communities throughout the state.
Finally, I wish to thank our Grand Master, Most Worshipful Larry L.
Adamson, for whom I have had the opportunity to work on so many significant
projects for our Grand Lodge this year.
Thank you.
Respectfully submitted,
Allan L. Casalou, Grand Secretary

Appendix F

Grand Lodge F. & A.M. of California

127

Report of the Grand Lecturer
To the Most Worshipful Grand Lodge, F.& A.M. of California:
California Masonic Code Section 404.250 directs me to make a report
to you at each Annual Communication, outlining the activities of the Ritual
Committee during the year just past and the condition of the Craft within this
jurisdiction. Before doing so, however, I must echo the sentiment with which
our Grand Master opens each of his addresses, expressing his gratitude for your
allowing him the privilege of serving our Fraternity. I too thank you for allowing
me to continue to try to carry on the legacy left by those who preceded me in this
office.
Much this year has been encouraging. I believe the quality of Masonic
education continues to improve. We still stress the role of the Officers’ Coaches
in teaching our ritual, and they have been exemplary in their assumption of that
responsibility. Our Grand Master repeatedly praises the number of candidates
we’ve enjoyed this year and we are thrilled by the number who elect to do fullform proficiencies. As is our custom, we’ve held two ritual conferences, one each
in the North and South, at which the Inspectors demonstrated their proficiency
in all three degrees. The Assistant Grand Lecturers hold additional schools for
the Inspectors and Officers’ Coaches. Also, Master Mason seminars to which
all Masons are invited. It has been my pleasure to attend and assist in several of
these. Of course, the Inspectors each conduct the ever popular District schools of
instruction, at which Officer attendance is mandatory. I continue to be impressed
by the dedication shown by all who have so willingly assumed the responsibility
for teaching our ritual.
This year saw another Ritual Competition, limited to individual lodges
and which focused on the Marshal’s interrogations, the perambulations and the
charges. We are pleased that the participation seems to be increasing. All of
the work was outstanding and every team should be applauded. We will present
awards to all Division finalists and the State winner, Natoma Lodge No. 64, at the
banquet on Saturday. I must admit, though, I was disappointed by Division VI.
Out of 30 lodges in the Division, not one showed the confidence and/or gumption
to enter a team.
We’ve had two important and exciting innovations this year. First, is the
advent of the IDA, the Inspector Development Academy. Under the chairmanship
of Vernon Dandridge, it was an intensive, three weekend workshop intended to
prepare prospective Inspectors to bring improved and expanded skills to the post.
It has been a resounding success and our Deputy Grand Master has selected 18 of
this years’ graduates to be appointed Inspectors.
A second major development has been to expand the Masonic Divisions
from five to nine, thus allowing us to welcome onto the Ritual Committee,

128

Proceedings of the

David Perry, Bruce Galloway, David Allmuth, Cub Jack and Jim Kurupas, and
to bring back an experienced hand, Jack Rose. As anticipated, this has resulted
in improved access to the Grand Lodge in the several areas, a more productive
level of supervision and ease of travel by the AGLs, and much better lines of
communication within the Divisions.
Pursuant to the requirements of Resolution 08-04, we developed ritual
for the installation of Assistant Lodge Secretaries. Additionally, we developed,
published and successfully implemented a ceremony for the rededication of
Masonic Temples or Centers.
We recently learned that the latest edition of the Officers’ Monitor
contains numerous errors. We are drafting a list of those errors, with corrections,
which will be distributed with the booklets. We plan to soon revise and republish
a whole new edition of that text.
Again, I must extend my deep and lasting thanks to my friends and
colleagues, the Inspectors and AGLs for their many hours of dedicated efforts
in protecting and perpetuating our ritual and its standards of perfection. Never,
for a minute, my Brethren, forget that all those efforts are for YOUR benefit.
We want you to be the very best conveyors of our lessons, and to guarantee a
quality education for our candidates. Without you, it would be impossible to
make Masons. We are saddened to report two of our Inspectors were taken from
us, Jim Adams and Bill McNeer. Both were conscientious, hard-working and
well-respected. They will be sorely missed.
Finally, Most Worshipful, June and I can’t thank you and Lynn enough
for making us a part of your Grand Family. It’s been a great year and we will
never forget your gracious leadership. Thanks also to Allan Casalou and the
entire Grand Lodge Office staff for your untiring, unstinting aid and assistance.
You have all been there to respond to our every need. And to you, my Brethren,
I can only extend my heartfelt gratitude for your dedication and devotion, and for
all you do to keep our gentle Craft growing and prospering. Thank you again for
the warmth and hospitality you have shown us on every occasion.
I’m going to conclude this report a little differently. I’ve decided what
I consider to be one of the most important lines in our Ritual. It’s one we’ve all
heard a thousand times, but one which doesn’t get the significance it deserves, so,
we’re going to have some audience participation. I’m going to ask some Masonic
questions and you’re going to give me the ritualistic answers. I expect to see
1,200 mouths move and hear 1,200 voices. This includes guests from our sister
jurisdictions, since I know you all have the same interchanges. At the opening of
each Second or Third Degree, the candidate is asked why he’s here. The Senior
Deacon, on his behalf, explains that he wants to advance, to gain greater light,
better understanding, to improve his position in Masonry and in life. The Master
and Wardens then ask how he expects to gain admission, to which the Deacon

Grand Lodge F. & A.M. of California

129

replies, “by (Esoteric Ritual).” The Master and Wardens inquire, “Has (Esoteric
Ritual)?” The Deacon answers, “He Has (Esoteric Ritual).” That, my Brethren, is
the pledge we make to all Masons, to help them fulfill their goals, and the pledge
we, as Masons, make to all society, to facilitate its improvement.
Thank you, my Brethren, for your help, and thank you for allowing me
to serve as your Grand Lecturer.
Respectfully submitted,
Paul D. Hennig, Grand Lecturer

Appendix G

Grand Lodge F. & A.M. of California

133

Grand Chaplain’s Message

by The Very Reverend Timothy A. Wood
Most Worshipful Grand Master, Past Grand Masters, Distinguised and Honored
Guets, my fellow Grand Lodge Officers and Brothers:
LIVING THE VIRTUES OF MASONRY AND BUILDING FOR A BETTER
TOMORROW
As we look back over the history of the United States, we do so with
reverence and admiration to the days of George Washington, Benjamin Franklin,
Paul Revere and the other founding fathers of our country. Early Masons provided
us with our moral compass, some fought and died for our liberty and freedom
and helped establish two of the world’s greatest documents in the Bill of Rights
and in the United States Constitution. Today, some may argue, but I believe it
is indisputable that our democracy and our way of life is the envy of the world
owing to early Masons following their dream of being Free Men and NOT living
under the threat of any King, or tyranny. They lived by the Words and Symbols of
Freemasonry and dedicated their lives to pursuing their Masonic responsibilities,
by following this path of moral and civic obligations, and adhering to the Masonic
charges given to them, they built a better tomorrow for each of us.
Brother Love, Faith, Relief, Charity
The Holy Bible, and the Square and Compass,
These are but a few of the symbols that we live by.
Additional symbols are highlighted in the public ceremonies of the laying
of a Cornerstone.
One of the better-known elements necessary for a successful cornerstone
is the “Casket” or otherwise known as the “Time Capsule”, this is a sealed container
placed inside a building’s foundation so that an eternal record may be found by
succeeding generations. Although this is an ancient, as well as modern tradition, it
is one that links the past with the present, and helps build toward the future.
Our founding fathers had the foresight and wisdom to place in our hands
a roadmap toward peace and happiness, moral conditions in the form of unshakable
virtues. They dreamed we could be better men, better toward our families and
neighbors and assume a civic responsibility in helping to build a better country.
They did not hide this secret away or bury it in a cement foundation; rather they
gave it to us as working tools to aid us along towards the future.
Buildings and people alike must be built on firm, solid foundations. A
building erected in sand, will collapse over time. A person without a moral compass

134

Proceedings of the

or ethical rules to live by will lack character and integrity and will ultimately fail;
Masons and Masonic families do not lack these necessary attributes.
We understand that everything we do as individuals is not only a reflection
of our moral character, it is a representation of this Fraternity; it is a reflection of
our home Lodge, and as a result, our actions are an expression of one another, they
are indeed a reflection of our forefathers who founded this country and who asked
us to be better men and better women working together toward the betterment of
our nation. Consequently, we continually do those things necessary to improve
our position in the community, and we consider our every action as a stepping
stone to that endeavor. The “Blue Lodge” is the heart, and it is the soul of our
Fraternity, it is where we make Masons, it is where we teach virtues, and create
desires in men to inspire others to become Masons. By continuing to practice the
tenets of Freemasonry in our daily lives, we have inspired others to do the same
as indicated by the fastest growing segment of our Fraternity of eighteen to thirty
year old men; today, people are watching us, and by observing our actions, they
are saying “Those Men Must Be Masons.”
A great fiery communicator can deliver eloquent words, but they will
soon be forgotten if not acted upon or followed. Masons are successful because
our words frame the foundation of our structural beliefs and Masons put them into
action. To them, to us, the laying of a Cornerstone is more than just a symbolic
act, it is a physical reminder that the building blocks or tenets of Freemasonry will
help erect a better tomorrow for man.
In 2004, Grand Master David Doan said, “Those of us who have chosen
to become Masons have committed ourselves to continuous self improvement.
This effort helps us get it right the first time and displays to the world the values
of Freemasonry which makes our communities a better place to live. We are
reminded that each of us can, and do, make a difference in the world by how we
act in our daily lives.”
Frederick L. Sorsabal, Past Grand Master in 2006 reminded us “we need
to be partners in progress so that our communities are informed and understand
that the blue lodge and other members of the family of Freemasonry are here for
their welfare and benefit.”
Past Grand Master Melvyn B. Stein said, “Masonry’s future depends
upon the present actions we take to meet the frontier of our vision: a vision that
insures continuing education and exploration into the relevance and value of
Masonic principles in our daily lives.”
Still, Past Grand Master, Richard Wakefield Hopper, called on us to
Stand Strong in Freemasonry. Today, we have programs in place like PASS IT
ON and ON THE LEVEL. We have Eastern Star, and our youth groups; Job’s
Daughters, Rainbow Girls and the DeMolay Boys.

Grand Lodge F. & A.M. of California

135

With the understanding and foresight of all of our Past Grand Masters,
together with the accomplishments of today, we have the programs necessary for
the building of a successful future, including our Grand Master’s project this year
to support Public Education as we have for the past 150 years. Effective this
year, Most Worshipful Adamson, through combined efforts with UCLA, have
established a program that will begin offering two classes dedicated to the Study
and Understanding of Freemasonry, and our impact on our country’s constitution
and democracy; indeed on our contribution toward the complexion of this great
nation. We could put this information into a Cornerstone and bury it somewhere.
We will not do that, instead we will bring our Masonic past and our future into
the open for all to view, appreciative and gracious for the opportunity, and content
with the dignified condition of our illustrious institution.
So, let us rededicate ourselves to not only to LIVING THE DREAM of
Freemasonry, but by actively participating in the direction of our beloved country
by following the leadership and wishes of our founding forefathers . . . and by
applying our Masonic tenets into our daily lives, we can and we will build for a
better tomorrow.
Soon, another Time Capsule or Casket will be going into the ground.
Another year completed and another one to look forward to. In the end, when
we analyze our efforts, let us pray that we will be satisfied with our own lifetime
achievements; that we will have given more than simply an “average” effort, and
that we will not reminisce about our regrets, rather we will stand tall and proud,
remembering the accomplishments and the contributions, which we have made
toward the betterment of humanity.
Winston Churchill said, “All that is necessary for the Triumph of Evil is
for good men to do nothing.” We, you have never done “nothing” . . . as Masons
and as Masonic families, you continue to change the world into a better place to
live . . . you make me proud to be a Mason!
I thank you for your time, and I especially thank you for listening. Thank
you very much.

Proceedings of the

Appendix H

Grand Lodge F. & A.M. of California

139

Grand Oration
WHO AM I?
In the beginning, or as my Rabbi (an avid baseball fan) has been known
to say: in the Big Inning, the earth was without form and void. And darkness
was upon the face of the deep. And the spirit of God moved upon the face of the
waters. And God said.... And God SAID: let there be light.
Most Worshipful Grand Master, Past Grand Masters, Distinguished and
Honored Guests, members of the Grand Lodge and Brothers all;
With these four words—Let there be light—there was light. By His
words, not by any action at all, God created the light by which we exist. By His
mere words alone, something as awesome, and dare I say, “earth-changing,” was
created out of absolute nothingness. Whether this allegory actually occurred, or is
simply metaphoric, it demonstrates that words really do matter. That much good,
and much harm, can come from words. And if we are truly created in the image
of the Supreme Architect, don’t our words also matter?
It is indeed an honor and a privilege to be able to share some of my
words and thoughts with you this day, during the 160th year of the Grand Lodge
of California.
It is my hope that at least some of what I have to impart will leave a deep
and indelible impression upon your mind. To this end, in your program book,
you have a blank page. On this page, I ask that you write three ideas that you
glean from this morning’s message. Just three things that you wish to change or
implement upon your return, to your lodge, your home or to your business.
Light has long been considered a symbol of creation. As we seek further
light in Freemasonry, perhaps we can ask ourselves, what are we creating? Is
each of us content with what we are designing upon our respective trestleboards?
I hope we are not.
We are going to do some calisthenics this morning. Don’t worry. We
won’t get your heart rate too high or cause you to break out in a sweat. Please
grasp your hands together like so. Yes, even you Most Worshipful. And you too,
Most Worshipfuls. Now, re-grasp your hands with the opposite forefinger on top.
Move your hands, up or down. How does that feel? Strange? Uncomfortable?
And what is your tendency? To leave your hands in that position? I didn’t think
so. In fact, I see that many of you have already allowed your hands to return to
that original, more comfortable position.
Now was that aerobic enough for you? Has your Grand Orator finally
gone off the deep end? Perhaps, but my hope is that this simple exercise will
help you to recognize and move from your comfort zone. For if we don’t change,

140

Proceedings of the

are unwilling to stretch and to grow, then we are slowly decaying, withering,
shrinking.
We sometimes think it is only our children who should be growing; and
I guess this is logical, since their growth is manifested so obviously, right before
of our eyes. We, on the other hand, tend to become ambivalent adults. But we all
seek to grow, to mature.
As leaders of our Craft, we must be the beacons of light. But we aren’t
simply moths seeking the light of a flame. It is more than just light or heat that
we need—we search for enlightenment. However, an illumined world costs more,
and requires more energy. And enlightenment requires deliberate effort. It takes a
constant yearning for self-improvement and continuous learning. Seeking further
light in Masonry should not cease once we become a Master Mason. It is our
further duty to enlighten our universe—to illuminate the paths for others; to “Hold
High the Torch of Freemasonry.” Are these statements too lofty? I hope they are
not.
Many people in today’s society seek the path of least resistance.
Masonry does not honor this approach, and in fact, Freemasonry challenges us to
take the correct path, regardless of the degree of resistance we might encounter.
As Masons, we have set ourselves apart from society as a whole. Are we better
than others, or just different? I submit to you that we had better be willing, and
able, to march on a higher level than society in general. If we do not, we are not
fulfilling the obligations each of us took at the altar. Our Masonic Code, as an
example, offers a higher authority than the law of the land. It requires us to act in
a proper and just manner in all our doings.
In this day when many of us lament the apparent demise of character,
scruples and personal responsibility of so many of our supposed leaders, what
can we do?
We must let society know that it can depend on us. And how do we do
this? Simply said: through our words, thoughts and deeds. As Grand Master
Adamson has challenged us all year, we must apply the teachings of Freemasonry
by living its virtues in our daily lives—outside of the lodge room. And when
we do this, we build for a better tomorrow. What we learn in the lodge room
benefits society as a whole. Michael Leon, editor of the New Zealand Freemason
stated that “our charge as Freemasons is to create real and positive change within
ourselves and within society.”
We are each like a tree—knowledge creates deep roots, but if we don’t
branch out, bud and bloom regularly by sharing ourselves with the world, then
our tree is void of life.
Each of us has our own impression, understanding and meaning that we
glean from Masonry. If you think about it, we belong to a pretty incredible society

Grand Lodge F. & A.M. of California

141

where men of such divergent backgrounds, experiences and opinions can join
and work together in a civilized manner. Freemasonry forbids the discussion in
Masonic meetings of religion, race, creeds, politics or other topics likely to excite
personal animosities. We require a 5/6 vote to change our Masonic Code because
we are a consensus-based fraternity. In fact, early Masonry may well have been
formed in an effort to bring men of opposing views together. We find a unity
among our diversity. I think this is more important today than ever. Perhaps,
by our example, we can begin to bring a degree of civility back into our own
communities.
We need to practice the art of Freemasonry. And as a Craft, we need
to evolve to reflect today’s society. What we do today will determine our
reputation and what Masons will have the ability to do tomorrow.
To paraphrase a certain political ad of last year’s interminable
presidential election campaign: It is 2 o’clock in the morning. You are startled
awake. Somebody is standing over you and asking—Who are you?
How do you answer? I am a man? A husband, a father? A Christian,
a Jew, a Muslim? I am an architect, an accountant, a truck driver, a firefighter?
An American? An Asian? An African American? A Marine? A veteran? A
tenor, an alto? A Mason? A… Wait a minute. A Mason?
How far down the list of “who are you” is the answer a Mason? I
suggest that if Mason wasn’t in the top few, then perhaps Masonry is not a
deep enough part of who you are. It isn’t engrained deeply enough so that the
Masonic ideals and tenets are embedded into your subconscious to the point
where you automatically react Masonically to life’s trials and tribulations.
Are you a rough ashlar? I hope so. You knocked on the door of
Freemasonry because you were a good man (maybe even a young good man)
who desired to become a better man. Not just a better person, but a better man.
The doors of Freemasonry were opened unto you, because you were
found to be worthy. My question to you is: Did you stop there? Perhaps as
Masons, we should make certain that we, at all times, work to improve who
we are, thus continuously perfecting the ashlar of which we are emblematic.
Ever remembering that it is not perfection for which we strive—but rather a
wholeness.
Perhaps your spiritual and emotional edifice is already standing on a
solid foundation, built on a strong cornerstone of Masonic principles. In which
case, I suggest looking for opportunities to patch any holes or dings and to apply
a fresh coat of paint to it.
How do you wear your Masonic ring? Obviously, one option is to
wear it so the symbol faces others, with the intent that prospective Masons may
recognize it and make further inquiry about our Craft. This is indeed noble,

142

Proceedings of the

as we should be proud of our membership in such an ancient and honorable
brotherhood and encourage others to inquire about it.
Or we can wear our ring so that the square and compasses face ourselves,
our hearts, our souls; thus serving as a constant reminder that we are Masons, and
as such, are to remember always to walk and act as one.
In fact, I have another exercise for you this morning. Why don’t you
remove your Masonic ring (if you are able) and put it back on the opposite way
for now. This may serve as a reminder of the true meaning behind the ring, and
may even help you to display it more proudly.
We consider ourselves to be speculative Masons. I suggest that we also
need to be operative Masons, in that we need to work with our hands as well as
our minds.
Our teachings emphasize personal responsibility and service to others.
The Jewish sage and scholar, Rabbi Hillel said:
If I am not for myself, who will be?
If I am only for myself, what am I?
And if not now, when?
In these three questions is the essence of Freemasonry today.
In order to gain proper perspective, we must be atop the hill. If looking
from a valley, the sun will rise late, and set early, and we aren’t able to see the
world about us. Opportunity is like a sunset. We had better grab it, for the moment
is fleeting. While we are fairly confident the sun will indeed rise again tomorrow,
will we be able to see it? Are we fulfilling our potential? What is your potential?
How about for your lodge?
So let us climb to the hilltop and examine our surroundings.
We are witnessing unprecedented interest in our Craft, especially
following the recent release of the novel The Lost Symbol by Dan Brown, who,
by the way, is not a Mason. This interest is evidenced by the number of Masonic
websites that crashed under the weight of hits following the release of the book.
However, while we are grateful that so many young men are finding their
way to Freemasonry, it is imperative that the world learn the truth about our Craft
from us, not from a novelist, as Most Worshipful has admonished us in the past.
We point to the fact that so many men are looking for a means to help them to
improve themselves through Masonry.
But I think we need to dig a bit deeper to learn what it is these men
are truly seeking and how we can best deliver. My brethren, watered down
Freemasonry-Lite is not who we are. And it will not quench the thirst of these

Grand Lodge F. & A.M. of California

143

young men.
Yet it will be easy to fall into this trap, both for the new candidate and for
us. Yes, we need to offer high tech, but we also need to offer high touch.
I wonder if, in this time of 24/7 communications, we know how to slow
down. How to appreciate the nuances and grandeur our ritual can offer. The
teachings of Freemasonry can help us to slow our pace and savor each day.
It is incumbent upon us to teach the young candidate the benefit of time,
patience and perseverance. We need to educate him as to the true purpose and
meaning behind our ritual.
And while we certainly need to immediately integrate our new brethren
into the lodge, I urge you to demonstrate that due restraint by not forcing a man
into the line as soon as he becomes a Master Mason. Allow him the opportunity
to observe and gain the benefits of brotherly love, and even an appreciation of our
beautiful ritual. By doing so, I think we will groom a better Mason, one who will
remain a stronger member of the lodge for the rest of his life.
Perhaps we should emulate the brethren of Oroville Lodge Number 103
when they place new candidate information on the lodge bulletin board, along
with his picture, so that the man may be personally welcomed every time he is
met.
We need to find ways to bridge the generations, all the while maintaining
the richness and purity of our heritage. One of the subliminal benefits to
Freemasonry is how the older and younger generations of Masons can learn and
grow from each other.
Let us whisper good counsel in our brothers’ ears, but always do so in
a respectful way. Former Grand Marshal, Jack Joe, of blessed memory, could
never allow me to exit the lodge room without catching up to me, putting his arm
around my shoulder, and gently informing me of what I had just done wrong.
This happened every meeting, no matter how well I though I had nailed the
ritual. At first, I was bothered, even offended by his overture. However, when I
finally allowed my ego to step aside, I learned to appreciate the good counsel he
was whispering in my ear. My brethren, I miss Worshipful Jack, and I miss his
wisdom.
For those of you who will be coming to the East to serve as Master of
your lodge, I encourage you to evaluate the personal weak points that you would
like to improve, and decide how you are going to allow your year in the oriental
chair to shape and develop you, as a man. For many of us, there is no better
opportunity for personal growth than when we are offered the occasion to rule and
govern our lodge. And ever remember that it is not the position that honors the
person, but rather the person that honors the position.

144

Proceedings of the

In this economy, it is more important than ever that we aid and assist
those who are less fortunate. The sign of a strong civil society is when it looks out
for the interests of its weakest citizens. It is therefore incumbent upon us fly to the
relief of a distressed worthy brother, his widow or orphans. Are we doing that?
Well, some of us are in fact flying to relief at supersonic speed. We are doing this,
right now, through our Masonic Homes and our Masonic Outreach Services.
Allow me to share just a few comments from recipients of assistance
through our MOS.
“Without MOS we would not be alive. We would be totally homeless
and our age and physical health could not survive that status. I cannot describe
the positive impact your support has on us, nor our endless gratitude that your
being there radiates. We never dreamed we would need you, but thank God you
are there!”
Another comment: “MOS support is a gift from heaven. Even though
it only covers the base essentials, one cannot survive without the basics of food
and shelter.”
Or: “There aren’t the words to express what a difference this has made.
For the past five years since my mom passed, I have seen my father cry regularly,
worry about money for food and medication, and in one instance, wanted to take
his life because he felt so helpless and hopeless. On Friday we received the first
check from MOS and he looked happy. He said ‘I feel like I’m dreaming.’ Then
he whistled and sang for the entire weekend. It was like a huge burden had been
lifted. It was a new lease on life for him, truly.”
And: “I can’t begin to imagine what I would do without MOS! I thank
God numerous times every day for the blessing MOS is in my life.”
My brethren, this is evidence of the difference you are making through
your contributions and support to the Masonic charities.
But this aid and support, by itself is not sufficient. Let us contact every
widow, and every brother who we have not seen in Lodge of late to see what
they might need from us. It may be pulling their overgrown weeds, building a
wheelchair ramp, or as simple as a periodic phone call or visit. If they need help,
but your lodge is unable provide the necessary support, I urge you to contact
Masonic Outreach Services. If you commit to making contact with these people,
I promise you will grow in the process.
My brethren, I pledge to walk with you on your way, if you so promise
to walk with me on mine. Let us not forget that we took an obligation from, and
to each other.
A lodge room is any place where we have two or more Masons acting
Masonically. And a lodge is like a symphony. One brother acting on his own

Grand Lodge F. & A.M. of California

145

produces a different, less appealing effect than the entire lodge acting in concert,
to produce beautiful results.
And each lodge is a community unto itself. It is easy for our lodges to
become insular, an island. We are much stronger as a group, supporting each
other, and taking advantage of certain economies of scale.
For as “Freemasonry is the foundation of our country, the blue Lodge
will always be the foundation of our Craft.”
May the door of our Lodge room be wide enough to receive all who
hunger for brotherly love; all who are lonely for fellowship.
May it welcome all who have cares to unburden, thanks to express,
hopes to nurture.
May the door of the Lodge room be narrow enough to shut out pettiness
and pride, envy and enmity.
May its threshold be no stumbling block to young or straying feet.
May it be too high to admit complacency, selfishness or harshness.
May this Lodge be, for all who enter, the doorway to a richer and more
meaningful life.
And may the Great Architect of the Universe, in His infinite wisdom,
make us know that the border of the lodge room is not the border of living, and
the walls of the lodge room are not shelters from the winds of truth, justice and
reality. Instead, we Masons, standing together, are that shelter.
So I ask you, what will you do to shape, hone, smooth, polish and perfect
your ashlar? For yourself, your family, community and for your lodge? Today,
tomorrow, and for generations to follow?
So that when you are startled awake in the middle of the night, and
somebody asks: Who are you? Your instinctive reply is: “I am a Mason.”
Thank you.
Respectfully submitted,
Russell E. Charvonia, Grand Orator

I want to especially thank our Senior Grand Deacon, Chris Holme, for
putting together the video portion of my presentation. A job well done Chris.

Grand Master’s Decision and Recommendation
Grand Master Adamson’s Decision No. 1
Guidelines for Use of Alcoholic Beverages
The sale and consumption of alcoholic beverages at Masonic activities
and on Masonic properties are governed by the laws of the State of California and
our California Masonic Code. Guidelines for the use of alcoholic beverages at
Masonic activities and on Masonic properties were approved by Grand Lodge in
1995 as Grand Master’s Recommendation No. 5. Since that time, the California
Department of Alcoholic Beverage Control has tightened its rules pertaining to
this subject. On January 27, 2009, I wrote all Lodge Masters, Lodge Secretaries,
Hall Association Presidents and Hall Association Secretaries with revised
alcoholic beverage guidelines to align them with current interpretation of the
laws of the State of California. Based on comments received after that mailing,
I have clarified some of the provisions of those guidelines and present in final
form in this decision.
Essentially, the revised guidelines proposed in this decision will prohibit
the sale and consumption of alcoholic beverages at Lodge and Hall Association
activities unless one of the following two conditions is met:
1.

The alcoholic beverages are donated by one person. Those in
attendance may not bring their own alcoholic beverages as such
would constitute a “bottle club” which requires a state liquor
license. Our law prohibits Lodges and Hall Associations from
holding a state liquor license.

2.

The alcoholic beverages are sold and served by a caterer with a
proper state liquor license.

These changes in the alcoholic beverage guidelines apply only to Lodges
and Hall Associations. The balance of the guidelines remains unchanged, and
they continue to apply to alcoholic beverage usage at Lodges, Hall Associations,
all prerequisite organizations and their Hall Associations.
If you approve this decision, the outdated alcohol beverage guidelines
now found in the appendix of the California Masonic Code in which the Grand
Master’s interpretative decisions are recorded will be replaced with the following
narrative:

150

Proceedings of the

Interpretation of Sections 811.010, 900.030 and 1200.045 of the California
Masonic Code.
Guidelines for Use of Alcoholic Beverages
by Masonic Lodges and Masonic Organizations
(1995 G.M. Recommendation No. 5,
as revised by 2009 G.M. Decision No. 1)
Questions frequently arise with respect to permissible use of alcoholic
beverages in Lodge halls, on other property owned by or leased to a Masonic
body and at Masonic activities held elsewhere. These guidelines explain the
Grand Lodge rules applicable to such use. These guidelines apply to Lodges,
Hall Associations, the Scottish Rite, the York Rite bodies, the Shrine, Eastern
Star and all other prerequisite organizations and their Hall Associations.
These guidelines address only the policy and regulations of the Grand
Lodge. Your use of alcoholic beverages also must comply with the applicable
laws of the State of California and the rules established by your Masonic Hall
Association or other landlord. The Scottish Rite, the York Rite bodies, the
Shrine, Eastern Star and other Masonic Organizations may also be subject to
their own organization’s regulations on alcohol.
A Masonic Lodge, Hall Association and a Masonic Organization may
allow alcoholic beverages to be served on Masonic property to their members
and invited guests, with one exception. Alcoholic beverages are not permitted in
a room where Masonic ceremonies are normally conducted unless that room is
also the regular social hall.
Lodges, Hall Associations and Masonic Organizations may maintain
a bar on Masonic property so long as alcoholic beverages are not regularly
or permanently stored in the bar. Alcohol should not be stored elsewhere on
Masonic property either.
Lodges and Hall Associations may not use funds from the Lodge’s or the
Hall Association’s treasury to purchase alcoholic beverages. No Lodge or Hall
Association funds may be used to reimburse anyone who has purchased alcoholic
beverages. Proceeds from ticket sales to a Lodge or Hall Association event may
never be used to purchase alcoholic beverages or to reimburse anyone who has
purchased alcoholic beverages. These restrictions apply only to Lodges and Hall
Associations. The Scottish Rite, the York Rite bodies, the Shrine, Eastern Star
and other Masonic Organizations may use funds from their own treasuries to
purchase alcoholic beverages and reimburse the purchase of alcoholic beverages
by others from their treasuries, if their regulations so permit. The Scottish Rite,
the York Rite bodies, the Shrine, Eastern Star and other Masonic Organizations
may use proceeds from ticket sales to an event to purchase the alcoholic beverages

Grand Lodge F. & A.M. of California

151

dispensed at the event or to reimburse anyone who has purchased alcoholic
beverages for the event, if their regulations so permit and if the alcohol is served
under a license from the California Department of Alcoholic Beverage Control
(ABC).
Alcoholic beverages may not be served, consumed, or sold during
the course of a meeting of a Masonic Lodge, Hall Association, or Masonic
Organization. Any alcoholic beverages must be removed from the meeting
room prior to beginning the meeting. The meeting may be opened and called
to refreshment, after which alcoholic beverages may be served, but the alcoholic
beverages must be removed from the meeting room prior to calling back to labor.
However, as noted above, alcoholic beverages are not permitted in a room where
Masonic ceremonies are normally conducted unless that room is also the regular
social hall.
Lodges and Masonic Organizations may hold tiled meetings and social
events on non-Masonic properties where there is a permanent bar, such as a
hotel or restaurant, provided that no alcoholic beverages are present during any
meeting. Any bar located elsewhere in the building may remain open during the
meeting.
Caterers with an ABC license may serve and sell alcoholic beverages at
all Masonic events held by any Lodge, Hall Association or Masonic Organization
on or away from Masonic property, if all applicable California laws are followed.
If the event sponsor is a Lodge or Hall Association, the caterer with an ABC
license and not the event’s sponsor must sell the tickets to the event.
No Lodge or Hall Association may directly or indirectly apply for,
assume title to or operate under any governmental license or permit for the
purpose of consuming, serving or selling alcoholic beverages, whether in, on
or away from premises owned, leased or rented by a Masonic Lodge or Hall
Association. Therefore, if alcoholic beverages are not sold and served by a
caterer with an ABC license, there are certain restrictions on the event which
must be observed because the event has not been licensed. As a general rule, no
license or permit is required if alcoholic beverages are not sold and the premises
are not open to the general public when alcoholic beverages are being served or
consumed.
●

If alcoholic beverages cannot be sold, you cannot have a cash bar or sell
drink tickets or tokens to be exchanged for alcoholic beverages. You
may charge for the meal or other event at which alcoholic beverages are
served, so long as the alcoholic beverages have been donated by one
person and everyone pays the same price, regardless of whether they
will be served an alcoholic beverage. Members and guests may not
bring their own alcohol as such would be a “bottle club” which requires
an ABC liquor license.

152
●

Proceedings of the
Under no circumstances may the general public be present when
alcoholic beverages are served without a license. You may not advertise
to the general public an event at which alcoholic beverages will be served
on Masonic property. Only members of the sponsoring Masonic body
and their invited guests may be in attendance when alcoholic beverages
are served on Masonic property by someone other than a caterer with an
ABC license. The term “general public” does not include the members
of the organization, their families or friends.

If alcoholic beverages are not sold and served by a caterer with an ABC
license, Masonic Organizations are subject to these same restrictions unless the
group is properly licensed by the California Department of Alcoholic Beverage
Control. This may include a single event license as well as a permanent club
license. Masonic Organizations, such as the Scottish Rite, the York Rite bodies,
the Shrine and Eastern Star, may hold such licenses, if their regulations so permit,
even though a Lodge and Hall Association may not hold such a license.
Lodges, Hall Associations and Masonic Organizations may rent or lease
their land or buildings to business establishments which serve and sell alcoholic
beverages as an incidental part of their businesses. For example, you may rent to
a restaurant which has a bar or to a grocery store that sells alcoholic beverages.
However, you may not rent to a bar business or a liquor store, even those bars and
liquor stores with incidental sales of non-alcoholic products.
Lodges, Hall Associations and Masonic Organizations may rent or lease
their land or buildings to non-Masonic tenants other than business establishments
who serve and sell alcoholic beverages, if the sponsor or caterer is properly licensed
by the California Department of Alcoholic Beverage Control. For example, you
may rent your building for a wedding reception or similar activity where alcoholic
beverages will be served and sold by a licensed sponsor or caterer.
As a reminder, activities of a Lodge, Hall Association or Masonic
Organization are always subject to Grand Lodge rules on commercialism and
permitted financial support, in addition to these guidelines.
When questions arise with respect to these Guidelines, contact your
Grand Lodge Committee on Masonic Properties.

Report of the Committee on Policy and General Purposes:
Our Grand Master’s Decision No. 1 recognizes the changes in rules
adopted by the California Department of Alcoholic Beverage Control as they relate
to the subject of the use of alcoholic beverages at Masonic activities and on Masonic
properties. His decision provides revised guidelines that, if approved, would
replace the outdated alcoholic beverage guidelines now found in the appendix of
the California Masonic Code.

Grand Lodge F. & A.M. of California

153

The Committee on Policy and General Purposes is of the opinion that
this decision is consistent with the policy and general purposes of this Grand
Lodge.
Decision AFFIRMED.
Grand Master Adamson’s Decision No. 2
Moral Turpitude
The California Masonic Code defines moral turpitude as follows:
§100.075. MORAL TURPITUDE:
Conduct contrary to generally accepted moral standards. It
includes any act of baseness, vileness, or depravity in the
private and social duties which a person owes to another
person, or to society in general, contrary to the accepted and
customary rule of right and duty between people. It also
includes conduct which is contrary to justice, honesty or good
morals, including acts of fraud, deceit and dishonesty, even
though not necessarily a crime.
The term “moral turpitude” is used in three places in the Code. A
Mason may not take a demit if a charge is pending under the criminal laws of
the United States or of any other governmental entity involving moral turpitude.
§§804.130 and 804.135 of the California Masonic Code. Unmasonic conduct
includes (1) any violation, before or after initiation, of the criminal laws of the
United States or of any other governmental entity involving moral turpitude and
(2) any action or failure to act in violation of the requirements of the Code, the
General Regulations of Masonry or the Ritual which involves a breach of faith
or other moral turpitude. §1401.010 of the California Masonic Code. If a Mason
has pleaded guilty or nolo contendere to, or has been convicted of, a crime of
moral turpitude by a federal or state court, he shall be deemed automatically
expelled from membership in any Lodge in this Jurisdiction without any further
action of his Lodge or Grand Lodge when the time for appeal of his conviction
has lapsed or the judgment of conviction has been affirmed on appeal. §1404.820
of the California Masonic Code.
I have been asked on a number of occasions to decide if certain conduct
involves moral turpitude under our law. With the help of the Grand Lodge
Committee on Jurisprudence, I have developed a list of such conduct. This list
has helped me decide when conduct does involve moral turpitude. Although it
would be nearly impossible for the list to be all inclusive of every possible conduct

154

Proceedings of the

involving moral turpitude, I believe that this list is generally comprehensive and
will help others in making similar determinations. I now present to you this list
in the form of a Grand Master’s Decision for publication in the appendix of the
California Masonic Code to help others on this issue.
If you approve this decision, the following narrative will be added
to the appendix of the California Masonic Code in which the Grand Master’s
interpretative decisions are recorded:
Interpretation of Section 100.075 of the California Masonic Code.
Moral Turpitude
(2009 G.M. Decision No. 2)
For conduct to involve moral turpitude, it normally must be accompanied
by an inherently evil intent. The most common examples of this inherently evil
intent are:
(1)
An intent to defraud;
(2)
An intent to take a thing unlawfully (larceny); and
(3)
An intent to harm a person or a thing.
Conduct involving moral turpitude can be broken down into three
general categories: actions against property, actions against people and actions
against government.
Actions Against Property
The following actions committed against property involve moral
turpitude:
(1)
Arson;
(2)
Blackmail;
(3)
Burglary;
(4)
Embezzlement;
(5)
Extortion;
(6)
False pretenses;
(7)
Forgery;
(8)
Fraud;
(9)
Larceny (grand or petty);
(10)
Malicious destruction of property;
(11)
Receiving stolen goods (with guilty knowledge);
(12)
Robbery;
(13)
Theft (if it involves the intention of permanent taking);
(14)
Transporting stolen property (with guilty knowledge);
and

Grand Lodge F. & A.M. of California
(15)

155

Similar actions.

The following actions against property do not involve moral turpitude:
(1)
Damaging private property (if intent to damage is not
present);
(2)
Breaking and entering (if no specific or implicit intent
to commit an act involving moral turpitude);
(3)
Passing bad checks (if intent to defraud is not present);
(4)
Possessing stolen property (if guilty knowledge is not
present);
(5)
Joy riding (if the intention to take permanently is not
present);
(6)
Juvenile delinquency; and
(7)
Similar actions.
Actions Committed Against Person, Family Relationship or Sexual Morality
The following actions committed against the person, family relationship
or sexual morality involve moral turpitude:
(1)
Abandonment of a minor child (if deliberate, voluntary
and with knowledge and if it results in the destitution
of the child);
(2)
Assault (which includes battery) (this action is broken
down into several categories, which involve moral
turpitude):
(a)
Assault with intent to kill;
(b)
Assault with intent to commit rape;
(c)
Assault with intent to commit robbery;
(d)
Assault with intent to commit serious bodily
harm; and
(e)
Assault with a dangerous or deadly weapon
(some weapons may be found to be dangerous
or deadly as a matter of law, while others
may or may not be found factually to be
such, depending upon all the circumstances
in the case; such circumstances may include,
but are not limited to, the size of the weapon,
the manner of its use, and the nature and
extent of injuries inflicted);
(3)
Bigamy
(4)
Trafficking in any controlled substance;
(5)
Contributing to the delinquency of a minor;
(6)
Gross indecency;
(7)
Incest (if the result of an improper sexual relationship);

(9)

Kidnapping;
Lewdness;
Manslaughter:
(a)
Voluntary; and
(b)
Involuntary, if done with recklessness, which
is defined as the awareness and conscious
disregard of a substantial and unjustified
risk which constitutes a gross deviation from
the standard that a reasonable person would
observe in the situation;
Mayhem;
Murder;

Prostitution;
Rape (by statute, a person may be convicted of

if she is under the statutory age at the time of the

Bestiality;
Possession of child pornography; and
Similar actions.

The following actions committed against the person, family relationship
or sexual morality do not involve moral turpitude:
(1)
Assault (simple) (i.e., any assault without an evil intent

(2)
Adultery;

Similar actions.

Actions Against Government
The following actions committed against governmental authority
involve moral turpitude:
(1)
Bribery;
(2)
Counterfeiting;
(3)
Fraud against revenue or other government functions;
(4)
Mail fraud;
(5)
Perjury;
(6)
Harboring a fugitive from justice (if with guilty
knowledge);
(7)
Tax evasion (if deliberate, voluntary and with
knowledge);
(8)
Terrorism;
(9)
Espionage; and
(10)
Similar actions.
Violation of laws which are regulatory in character and do not include
an element of fraud or other evil intent do not involve moral turpitude. The
following actions do not involve moral turpitude, unless in violation of a law
which requires an intent to defraud or commit other evil:
(1)
Black market violations;
(2)
Breach of the peace;
(3)
Carrying a concealed weapon;
(4)
Disorderly conduct;
(5)

(18)
Vagrancy; and
(19)
Similar actions.

Attempts, Aiding and Abetting, Accessories and Conspiracy
The following actions involve moral turpitude:
(1)
An attempt to commit an act involving moral
turpitude;
(2)
Aiding and abetting in the commission of an act
involving moral turpitude;
(3)
Being an accessory (before or after the fact) in the
commission of an act involving moral turpitude;
(4)
Taking part in a conspiracy (or attempting to take part
in a conspiracy) to commit an act involving moral
turpitude; and
(5)
Similar actions.
Report of the Committee on Policy and General Purposes:
In Grand Master’s Decision No. 2, Grand Master Adamson expands
and clarifies existing language in the California Masonic Code pertaining to
moral turpitude. The new language lists examples of moral turpitude without
excluding those which may be of a generic nature that are not spelled out.
This is intended to give the charging party more guidance in framing charges,
and the trial master and trial commission a clearer understanding of alleged
violation of our duty as Masons.
Since this legislation clarifies and expands on existing legislation, it is
consistent with the policy and general purposes of Grand Lodge.
Report of the Committee on Trial Review:
The Masters of our constituent Lodges, as well as our Trial Masters,
Trial Commissions and the Trial Review Committee are, from time to time,
called upon to make a determination as to whether certain criminal conduct
constitutes moral turpitude as that term is defined in Section 100.075 of the
California Masonic Code. This Decision provides a useful resource to aid
them in making that determination and also presented a reference for any
Brother who may want to satisfy himself as to whether certain criminal conduct
constitutes moral turpitude as defined in oour Code.
The Trial Review Committee fully supports the adoption of this
Decision.
Decision AFFIRMED.
