Grand Lodge F. & A.M. of California

195

Report of the Committee on Policy and General Purposes:
Last year Grand Master Hopper recommended that the California
Masonic Code be amended to provide a clearer definition of unmasonic conduct
as it relates to hazing in our Fraternity. He pointed out that some of this hazing
exists prior to the conferral of degrees, and sometimes during a degree itself.
Hazing has no place in our Craft. The ritual of our Jurisdiction has no room for
physical or mental injury or humiliation of a candidate and is not something that
is taught by the Ritual Committee. Grand Master Hopper in this recommendation
clearly defined the meaning of hazing and more purposely determined that it will
not be allowed in this Jurisdiction.
As the committee reported last year, this resolution is consistent with the
policy and general purposes of this Grand Lodge.

Report of the Committee on Ritual:
Hazing by definition is initiating candidates by means of horseplay,
practical jokes, and tricks which is often in the nature of humiliating or painful
ordeals. Hazing has the capacity to degenerate into incidents where candidates
can be treated in a manner that does not reflect positively on our intent to teach
important lessons of brotherly love, mutual respect for each other and trust in our
integrity to treat each other honorably. As such, this recommendation is Grand
Lodge’s effort to take an official stand against any activity that would cause
intentional infliction of physical or mental injury or humiliation on a candidate,
preliminary to, during or following a degree.
The Ritual Committee supports this recommendation.
Recommendation APPROVED.
Resolution No. 08-08
Distributions From Life Membership Funds
The proponents of this Resolution seek to change the manner in which the optional
annual distribution from a Life Membership investment account is calculated.
To the Most Worshipful Grand Lodge, F.& A.M. of California:
WHEREAS, widely accepted investment best practices are available to
be applied to the investment of a Lodge’s Life Membership fund and are allowed
under the existing provisions of the California Masonic Code; and

196

Proceedings of the

WHEREAS, prudent investment theory endorses a conservative
investment program with an objective of total return, accompanied by a sound
distribution policy.
NOW, THEREFORE, BE IT RESOLVED that the Section 809.150 of
the California Masonic Code be amended as marked:
§809.150. LIFE MEMBERSHIP FUND.
The original amounts paid for life memberships shall be forever
kept and maintained as and for a separate and permanent Life
Membership Fund. A Lodge may invest its Life Membership
Fund as provided in Section 809.130 of this Code, if such
investments are kept separate and apart from all other Lodge
assets. Only interest or dividends paid in cash on An annual
amount up to four percent of the prior year-end fair market
value of the assets held in the Life Membership Fund shall be
available for general Lodge use.
Report of the Committee on Policy and General Purposes:
The proponents of resolution 08-08 are seeking to change the method by
which income from a Lodge Life Membership Fund can be distributed. Under
the current regulations, a Lodge may only spend the interest and dividends that
that fund earns. This has potential, and, in some lodges, real consequences with
regard to budgeting. Since dividend income and interest rates can fluctuate,
sometimes widely, from year to year, it is impossible for any Lodge to know
exactly how much money to include in its annual budget.
The proposed revision, namely allowing the Lodge to budget up to 4%
of the total Life Membership Fund, would allow the Lodge to know specifically
at the start of any Masonic year how much money would be available. This rate is
one that institutional investors in non-profit charities have employed. The basic
assumption that these investors make is that an investment portfolio will earn, on
average, 7% per year. They allow 4% to be spent and leave the remaining 3% in
the fund to offset inflation.
From a practical perspective, if the investment return in any year is
less than 4% and the Lodge chose to spend the upper permissible limit, the total
amount in the fund would decrease in that year. However, in years when the
investment return is above 4%, that total amount would increase. Over a long
period of time, it would be expected that the value of the Lodge Life Membership
Fund would remain stable even when adjusted for inflation thereby insuring that
the support derived from the fund would continue indefinitely, which was the
intent of the brethren who purchased life memberships.

Grand Lodge F. & A.M. of California

197

As this committee stated last year, this resolution is consistent with the
policy and general purposes of this Grand Lodge.
Resolution FAILED.
Resolution No. 08-09
Increasing the Fee Paid by Applicants to the
George Washington Masonic Memorial
The proponents of this Resolution seek to increase the fee paid by applicants to
the George Washington Masonic Memorial.
To the Most Worshipful Grand Lodge, F.& A.M. of California:
WHEREAS, the George Washington Masonic Memorial located in
Alexandria, Virginia, has continually represented the Masons of the United States
since its dedication in 1932 and has overlooked our Nation’s Capital exhibiting all
facets of Masonry to countless visitors since it’s opening; and
WHEREAS, the age of the memorial demands increasing levels of
maintenance and requires an assured level of funding to continue to accomplish
its mission; and
WHEREAS, all Master Masons in the United States are members of the
George Washington Masonic National Memorial Association by virtue of their
membership in a constituent Lodge in their respective jurisdictions; and
WHEREAS, the only source of funding for the George Washington
Masonic Memorial is through donations by Grand Lodges, Lodges and individuals;
and
WHEREAS, the Grand Lodge of California currently supports the
George Washington Masonic Memorial be a contribution of $5.00 paid by each
applicant for the degrees of Masonry; and
WHEREAS, this donation of $5.00 per applicant has been our practice
and at the same rate for many, many years; and
WHEREAS, applicants for the degrees of Masonry in this jurisdiction
should contribute more than a nominal amount to the future of this magnificent
memorial to our First President whose dedication to Freemasonry set this country
on the path of liberty and freedom.
NOW, THEREFORE, BE IT RESOLVED, that Section 804.265 of
the California Masonic Code be amended as marked:

198

Proceedings of the

§804.265. NATIONAL MEMORIAL AND MEMORIAL
TEMPLE CONTRIBUTIONS.
Each applicant for the degrees shall contribute $15 $5 for the
Endowment Fund of the George Washington Masonic National
Memorial and $9 for the California Masonic Memorial Temple,
unless he shall have paid such contributions with a former
application to a Lodge of this Jurisdiction. Any contribution
required under this section shall accompany the application
or it cannot be received by the Lodge. All such contributions
shall be transmitted by the Lodge to the Grand Secretary at the
time and in the manner specified on forms provided for that
purpose. The Grand Secretary shall pay the same over to the
Grand Treasurer to be held subject to allocation and payment by
order of the Grand Master.
If the application is rejected or withdrawn, the Lodge shall
return any such contribution paid to the applicant. Such
contributions shall be non-refundable upon such applicant’s
election to receive the degrees.

Report of the Committee on Policy and General Purposes:
The proponents of resolution 08-09 are seeking to increase the fee paid
by applicants to the George Washington Masonic Memorial. Currently under the
CMC section 804.265 “candidates for Masonic degrees contribute $5.00 to the
Endowment Fund of the George Washington Masonic National Memorial.” The
proponents of Resolution 08-09 have stated that the age of the memorial demands
increasing levels of maintenance and requires an assured level of funding to
continue to accomplish its mission. Furthermore, the only source of funding for
the memorial is donations by Grand Lodges, Lodges and individuals.
The proponents of this resolution propose to amend the CMC section
804.265 to read; “Each applicant for the degrees shall contribute $15 for the
Endowment Fund of the George Washington Masonic National Memorial.”
The change from $5 to a total contribution of $15 per applicant will be
the first time this contribution has changed since the year 1980.
As this committee stated last year, we find that this resolution is consistent
with the policy and general purposes of this Grand Lodge.
Resolution ADOPTED.

Resolution No. 09-01
Creates the Hiram’s Pillar Award
The proponents of this Resolution seek to create the designation of Hiram’s Pillar
for the loved one of a Hiram Award recipient.
To the Most Worshipful Grand Lodge, F.& A.M. of California:
WHEREAS, the Hiram Award is given to Brethren who have consistently
and selflessly given invaluable service and time to their Lodges and the Fraternity;
and
WHEREAS, Brethren who receive the Hiram Award in recognition
usually do so with the incredible support of a loved one; and
WHEREAS, that loved one rarely receives the recognition they richly
deserve for their equally selfless support of and sacrifice to the recipient and his
Lodge.
NOW, THEREFORE, BE IT RESOLVED, that Section 408.020 of
the California Masonic Code be amended as marked:
§408.020. HIRAM AWARDS.
The Grand Lodge, upon request of a Constituent Lodge under its jurisdiction,
shall furnish a certificate honoring the Master Mason designated by the Lodge as
a recipient of the Hiram Award. The certificate shall be delivered to the Lodge for
presentation to the recipient. The Grand Lodge shall make available for purchase
a medallion and lapel pin for recipients of the Hiram Award.
The Lodge may only designate for the Hiram Award a Master Mason who has
served the Lodge and the Masonic Fraternity with devotion over and above the
ordinary. Service as an officer or as Master of a Lodge shall not be in itself
sufficient qualification for the award nor shall it be a disqualification. The award
should be based on what the recipient has done, not what positions he has held.
The Hiram Award shall be considered the highest honor for service that can be
bestowed upon a member by a Constituent Lodge. The presentation should be
made with appropriate ceremony. When a selection has been made, it should be
announced so the recipient may invite family and friends to the award presentation.
To maintain the prestige and importance of the Hiram Award, a Lodge may
present only one Hiram Award in any one calendar year. Such limitation should
not suggest that this award should be presented every year.

200

Proceedings of the

The Grand Lodge, upon request of a Hiram Award recipient, shall furnish a
certificate honoring an individual designated by such recipient as a Hiram’s Pillar.
The certificate shall be delivered to such recipient’s Lodge for presentation to
the individual so designated as a Hiram’s Pillar. The Grand Lodge shall make
available for purchase a pin for those designated as Hiram’s Pillars.

Report of the Committee on Policy and General Purposes:
The Hiram Award is to honor those who have given of their time, talent
and treasure to sustain the values, principles and service of our Lodges. This
resolution seeks to create the designation of Hiram’s Pillar Award for the loved
one of a Hiram Award recipient.
The wife of a Hiram Award recipient is normally presented with a
bouquet of flowers at the time of the award. While this is an inspiring moment,
the flowers do not have a permanent value. Thus, one of the reasons for this
Resolution is a more lasting memento of this most auspicious event. However,
the language of this Resolution states that the Hiram Award recipient shall request
the Hiram’s Pillar Award. Our Grand Lodge Policy on awards is to respond to
requests from Constituent Lodges and not from individual members.
For this reason, this Resolution is not consistent with the policy and
general purposes of this Grand Lodge.
Resolution CARRIED OVER.

of officers of the Lodge, which in some cases requires a lodge to adopt a budget

§805.230. DUTIES OF SECRETARY.

§809.000. LODGE FISCAL YEAR.
For financial reporting and budgeting purposes, a Lodge shall use a fiscal year

BE IT FURTHER RESOLVED, that Section 809.510 of the California
Masonic Code be amended as marked:
§809.510. LODGE BUDGET.
A Lodge shall adopt a budget for the following 12 months each fiscal year no
later than at its January at the first Stated Meeting for that fiscal year held after the
annual installation of the officers of the Lodge.

204

Proceedings of the

Report of the Committee on Policy and General Purposes:
The proponents of this Resolution seek to “Simplify and Clarify” Lodge
financial reporting and dates.
To simplify they have proposed changes to our code, section 805.230
(Duties of the Secretary). In that section they would eliminate the ‘semi-annual
financial report’ dated June 30th that is currently required to be sent to Grand
Lodge. They state that it is sufficient enough for a lodge to make one annual report
to the Grand Lodge for the fiscal year ending December 31st.
It should be noted that the language in section 805.230 (B) was not
changed. What that means is that lodges would still be required to make a ‘semiannual financial report’ ending June 30th, to the membership at the stated meeting.
This proposal would add a new section 809.000 to help clarify the
dates intended for annual reports. To ‘clarify’ they note that our code refers to an
annual report but it does not state the exact dates. It has always been for reporting
purposes, January 1st – December 31st. This proposal will codify that fact.
In Section 809.510 (Lodge Budget), they have re-worded this section to
help clarify that the budget shall be adopted for the fiscal year. This section would
now clarify that the calendar year is the fiscal year.
This legislation is consistent with the policy and general purposes of the
Grand Lodge of California.
Resolution ADOPTED.
Resolution No. 09-03
Expands Maximum Size and Composition of
Grand Lodge Committee on Investments
The proponents of this Resolution seek to change the maximum number of
members who can be appointed to serve on the Grand Lodge Committee on
Investment from nine to twelve and to allow up to 30% of the committee to be
California Master Masons who are not members of Grand Lodge.
To the Most Worshipful Grand Lodge, F.& A.M. of California:
WHEREAS, managing the investment portfolios of the Grand Lodge of
California, Masonic Homes of California and other Masonic entities has grown
more complex and will require members having a greater degree of investment
experience as well as expertise in one investment method; and

Grand Lodge F. & A.M. of California

205

WHEREAS, the Committee on Investment will require more
involvement from members of the Committee to adequately supervise and monitor
the various investment managers and consultants hired by the Grand Lodge; and
WHEREAS, the Committee on Investment will require more flexibility
in determining the size and composition of the Committee.
NOW, THEREFORE, BE IT RESOLVED, that Section 406.045 of
the California Masonic Code be amended as follows:
§406.045. INVESTMENTS.
The Committee on Investments shall consist of not less than five nor more than
nine twelve members. It shall invest and reinvest the funds of Grand Lodge and
any other entity thereof or under control of Grand Lodge, except Constituent
Lodges, Masonic Hall Associations, and Masonic Cemeteries. All such activities
shall require a majority number of the members to be present as a quorum.
The Committee shall consist of nine members appointed by successive Grand
Masters from among the members of Grand Lodge with experience in the broad
field of investments. Each Grand Master shall designate one of the members
of the Committee as chairman and another as vice‑chairman to serve during the
tenure of the Grand Master and until a successor, in that capacity, is designated.
Each Grand Master shall may also appoint three no more than one-third of the
authorized number of members to the Committee to serve thereon for a three
year term. Any vacancy occurring on the Committee shall be filled by the Grand
Master, the appointee to serve thereon for the remainder of the unexpired term
of his predecessor. The Grand Master may appoint the Grand Treasurer or
representatives of any Grand Lodge entity to the Committee.
The Grand Secretary shall serve as Secretary of the Committee, attend its meetings,
keep its minutes, and handle its correspondence but shall not have a vote unless
he is an appointed member. If the Grand Treasurer and the Chairman of the
Committee on Finance are not members of the Committee, they shall be given
ample notice of the meetings of the Committee and may attend and participate,
but shall not have a vote.

206

Proceedings of the

Report of the Committee on Policy and General Purposes:
The proponents of this Resolution seek to change the maximum
number of members who may be appointed to the Grand Lodge Committee
on Investments from nine (9) to twelve (12) to more adequately monitor and
supervise the various investment managers and financial consultants hired by
the Grand Lodge.
This Resolution will enable the Investment Committee to have
members who will be able to become more actively involved with the financial
consultants and investment managers as the Grand Lodge of California, the
Masonic Homes of California and other Masonic entities have become more
complex. The increase of members will provide the Investment Committee
with more expertise in “modernizing” the portfolio which they oversee along
with more consistent and frequent monitoring of the consultants. This task
has become one of the outputs for the committee as the Grand Lodge does not
have the staff to provide this oversight. This Resolution would also provide
the Investment Committee an opportunity to broaden the membership with
members with this type of financial background as the Grand Lodge portfolio
becomes more complex.
This Resolution does not change the manner in which the members of
the Committee are appointed. This Resolution is consistent with the policy and
general purposes of this Grand Lodge.
Resolution ADOPTED.
Resolution No. 09-04
Requires Member Retention Committee Interview
Before Suspension for Nonpayment Of Dues
The proponents of this Resolution seek to require the Member Retention
Committee to interview a member in an effort to retain him before he can be
suspended for nonpayment of dues.
To the Most Worshipful Grand Lodge, F.& A.M. of California:
WHEREAS, member retention is not only critical to the survival of
our fraternity but enables us to fulfill our Masonic obligations to our brother
Master Masons; and
WHEREAS, every year 1/5% to 2% of our members are suspended
for nonpayment of dues; and
WHEREAS, far too often long standing members are suspended for
nonpayment of dues at a time in their lives when they need our assistance the

Grand Lodge F. & A.M. of California

207

most, yet their suspended status makes them ineligible for programs like the
Masonic Outreach Services or admission into the Masonic Homes in Union
City and Covina; and
WHEREAS, Section 806.000.C of the California Masonic Code states,
“The Master, at the Stated Meeting next succeeding his installation, shall appoint
a Member Retention Committee to personally, or otherwise, interview each
delinquent member, ascertain the cause, and work closely with the Master and
officers in a continuing effort to effect the retention of all deserving members
unable to pay”; and
WHEREAS, if the Lodge Member Retention Committee were required
to interview a member before he could be suspended for nonpayment of dues we
would be better enabled to retain a worthy brother and, if necessary, provide him
relief.
NOW, THEREFORE, BE IT RESOLVED, that Section 809.370 of
the California Masonic Code be amended as follows:
§809.370. SUSPENSION FOR NON-PAYMENT OF DUES.
If a member has not paid his dues during a period of 12 months, the Secretary shall
notify him that he will be suspended subject to suspension for non-payment of dues
unless at the Lodge’s next Stated Meeting within thirty days either his dues have
been paid or sickness or inability to pay is shown as the cause for the failure to pay.
The notice shall be given either by certified mail, with return receipt requested,
addressed to his last known address, or by actual service of notice, duly certified by
the Tiler. The Secretary shall promptly provide a copy of the notice to the Lodge’s
Member Retention Committee which shall interview the delinquent member and
evaluate whether he is deserving and unable to pay. At the Stated Meeting following
the end of the thirty day period, the Member Retention Committee shall submit its
report on the delinquent member to the Lodge. If all dues due and payable have
not been paid by the time of the Lodge’s next that Stated Meeting, he the delinquent
member shall be declared suspended by the Master after the Lodge has received the
report on the member from the Member Retention Committee, unless for special
reasons shown the Lodge shall remit his dues or grant him further time for payment.
A member shall not be expelled from Masonry for non-payment of dues.

Report of the Committee on Policy and General Purposes:
Throughout the years, Grand Lodge has consistently affirmed the
importance of membership retention. Several resolutions have been proposed in
previous years which would make it easier for Lodges to notify members of their
delinquent dues and to suspend them; none were adopted.

208

Proceedings of the

Section 806.000 of the California Masonic Code states that the Member
Retention Committee should “personally, or otherwise, interview each delinquent
member, ascertain the cause and work closely with the Master and officers in a
continuing effort to effect the retention of all deserving members unable to pay”.
However, Section 809.370 does not require that such an interview occur before the
Lodge votes on suspending a delinquent member.
This resolution would reconcile these sections by requiring that the
Member Retention committee perform its duties under Section 806.000 before a
member could be suspended and is consistent with the policy and general purposes
of this Grand Lodge.
Resolution NOT ADOPTED.
Resolution No. 09-05
Allows Return of Unused Dues Without Lodge Consent
The proponents of this Resolution seek to allow the payment of unused dues,
without Lodge consent, of a deceased Mason or a Mason who has discontinued his
membership.
To the Most Worshipful Grand Lodge, F.& A.M. of California:
WHEREAS, Section 809.350 of the California Masonic Code requires
the return of dues in the event of the member’s death, demit, or discontinuance of
his multiple membership; and
WHEREAS, Section 809.520 of the California Masonic Code does not
include the return of dues as an exception to the requirement of Lodge consent
before an expenditure may be made; and
WHEREAS, the requirement to obtain the consent of the Lodge causes a
delay of up to a month in returning the dues to the member or his heirs.
NOW, THEREFORE, BE IT RESOLVED, that Section 809.520 of the
California Masonic Code be amended as marked:
§809.520. EXPENDITURES.
Lodge funds are trust funds set apart for the payment of the necessary, discretionary
and social expenses of the Lodge and for the special calls for charity.
No Lodge funds shall be expended without the express consent of the Lodge, even
though included in a Lodge budget, except that funds may be expended without
Lodge consent when:

Grand Lodge F. & A.M. of California

209

A.

Dispensed by the Charity Committee;

B.

Due Grand Lodge; or

C.

Required to pay the taxes or other charges of local, State or Federal
governmental agencies.; or

D.

Required under Section 809.350 of this Code as a return of dues.

Lodges may approve bills against the Lodge only when the bills are supported by
proper documentation, such as an invoice or receipt. No warrant shall be drawn
unless there are sufficient funds to honor it.

Report of the Committee on Policy and General Purposes:
California Masonic Code Section 809.350 states “If a member dies, is
issued a Demit, or discontinues his multiple membership the Lodge shall refund
to his authorized successor in interest or to him dues paid in advance of the month
when he died or his membership terminated.” The proponents of Resolution 0905 have observed that there is a delay in the process of returning these residual
monies because of the need to bring the matter to a Stated Meeting and have
the Lodge grant formal approval. Particularly in the event of death, the lack of
a timely return of the money creates an appearance of poor administration and
can potentially create further grief in the family a month or so after the brother’s
demise. The solution being proposed is a change in the wording of CMC section
809.520, the section that deals with permitted expenditures without the express
consent of the Lodge. The change would add, as a category, all three categories
of membership cessation described in section 809.350.
The resolution does not change CMC section 809.350, which
requires that a brother who ceases membership in a particular Lodge by demit
or discontinuation of multiple membership is required to surrender his dues
certificate prior to a receipt of the residual dues money. More importantly, the
resolution does not change the wording of CMC sections 804.130 or 804.160.
These sections deal with the process of obtaining a demit or a withdrawal of a
multiple membership. In both sections, it is explicitly stated that these cessations
of membership in a particular Lodge must occur at a Stated Meeting. As such,
the demit or withdrawal does not exist until the approval of it at the very Stated
Meeting that the proponents are trying to circumvent. Changing only the wording
of section 809.520 will not provide the early return of the owed membership dues
in these two circumstances. Thus, the resolution would only accelerate the time
when dues can be returned in the event of a member’s death. Of course, this is the
most sensitive circumstance regarding returning of dues.

210

Proceedings of the

As such, even with this limitation, the resolution is consistent with our
policies and general purposes.
Resolution CARRIED OVER.
Resolution No. 09-06
Acknowledges Former Masters During the Opening of Research Lodges
The proponents of this Resolution seek to allow the Master of a Research Lodge
to invite former Research Lodge Masters to seats in the East.
To the Most Worshipful Grand Lodge, F.& A.M. of California:
WHEREAS, Research Lodges open all their meetings on the Third
Degree; and
WHEREAS, Masters of Research Lodges upon completion of their term
of office are not entitled to the benefits or title of a Past Master; and
WHEREAS, it is only fair and proper that their Research Lodge
acknowledge their service as former Masters; and
WHEREAS, regular Blue lodges as well as Research lodges properly
and ritually welcome all Masters and Past Masters to seats in the East; and
WHEREAS, no such recognition is presently made in Research Lodges
recognizing former Masters.
NOW, THEREFORE, BE IT RESOLVED, that when opening a
Research Lodge the wording of the invitation to seats in the East shall be changed
from, “All Masters and Past Masters are cordially invited to seats in the East” to
“All Masters, Past Masters and Former Masters, are cordially invited to seats in
the East.” This change shall only apply to the opening of Research Lodges.

Report of the Committee on Policy and General Purposes:
The proponents of this Resolution seek to allow the Master of a Research
Lodge to invite former Research Masters to seats in the East. These Research
Lodges perform a valuable function in the discussion, preservation of Masonic
information and conducting programs that are of general service to the Craft.
They are a special classification of Constituent Lodges in this Grand Lodge.
Per Section 814.000 (G) of the California Masonic Code, when a Masterelect or a Warden-elect or a Senior Deacon appointed is not a Past Master of
a California Lodge, he must receive a certificate of proficiency in the opening

Grand Lodge F. & A.M. of California

211

and closing ceremonies. Paragraph 814.000 (J) states that “Sections of this Code
dealing with the title of Past Master shall not be applicable to one who has served
as Master of a Research Lodge only, …” Our Grand Lodge has perpetually
honored the Past Masters for their service and Ritualistic Proficiency in all of the
Degrees.
This Resolution is not consistent with the policy and general purposes of
this Grand Lodge.

Report of the Committee on Ritual:
Brethren of Grand Lodge, the proponents of this resolution seek to
modify our ritual during the opening of a Research Lodge to say, “All Masters,
Past Masters and Former Masters, are cordially invited to seats in the East.” This
change is to apply only to the opening of Research Lodges and not to the opening
of a regularly constituted blue Lodge. The Masters of Research Ldoges, although
they don’t receive the title of Past Master, have still dedicated themselves to the
running of the Lodge and the performance of their duties. The Ritual Committee
recognizes the desire to show respect to Former Masters of Research Lodges and
to acknowledge their contributions to that Lodge. The Ritual Committee does not
oppose this resolution.
Resolution CARRIED OVER.
Resolution No. 09-07
Allows a Brother to Serve as Secretary of More Than One Lodge
The proponents of this Resolution seek to allow a Master Mason to serve as
Secretary of more than one lodge.
To the Most Worshipful Grand Lodge, F.& A.M. of California:
WHEREAS, the Secretary plays a vital role in the administration of a
lodge; and
WHEREAS, there are essential skills and experience necessary for a
brother to effectively perform the duties of a Secretary; and
WHEREAS, not every lodge is able to find a qualified brother to
willingly serve as Secretary; and
WHEREAS, technology has streamlined many of the important
processes that a Secretary manages; and

212

Proceedings of the

WHEREAS, the California Masonic Code provides for the appointment
of an Assistant Secretary and such appointment provides for a more experienced
Secretary to train a brother for sufficient time in order that he might assume the
role of Secretary in the future; and
WHEREAS, some brothers are capable of serving as Secretary of
more than one Lodge and some Lodges could benefit from the service of these
brothers; and
WHEREAS, the California Masonic Code §805.010 currently does not
allow one brother to be the Secretary of more than one lodge at the same time
even though this may be necessary and the qualified brother may be available
for such service.
NOW, THEREFORE, BE IT RESOLVED, that Section 805.010 of
the California Masonic Code be amended as marked:
§805.010. OFFICERS.
The officers of a Lodge are Master, Senior Warden, Junior Warden, Treasurer,
Secretary, Senior Deacon, Junior Deacon, Marshal, Senior Steward, Junior
Steward, Tiler and other officers as the Lodge may deem proper to appoint.
The Master, Senior Warden and Junior Warden, Treasurer and Secretary shall
be elected in conformity with this Code. The other officers shall be appointed
by the Master. Any Master Mason in good standing, whether or not a member
of the Lodge, may be appointed Tiler or Organist. All other officers shall be
members in good standing of the Lodge.
A member may not be Master, or a Warden or Secretary, or any combination
thereof, of more than one Lodge at the same time.
The title of Master applies not only to him who has been elected and installed as
Master, but to either of the Wardens while acting as Master.

Report of the Committee on Policy and General Purposes:
California Masonic Code Section 805.010 currently does not allow one
brother to be the Secretary of more than one lodge at the same time. “A member
may not be Master, Warden or Secretary, or any combination thereof, of more
than one Lodge at the same time.” The proponents of Resolution 09-07 have
stated that not every lodge is able to find qualified brothers to willingly serve
as Secretary. The resolution allows brothers who are qualified and available to
serve more than one Lodge.

Grand Lodge F. & A.M. of California

213

There are potential adverse consequences of this legislation. Having
the same Brother serve as Secretary of multiple Lodges creates potential
conflicts of interest and undermines the autonomy of each individual Lodge.
Perhaps more importantly, the current section of our code does provide a
solution for the problem that concerns the proponents. It allows for appointments
of “Assistant Secretaries”, not only to help Secretaries, but, more importantly, to
provide an avenue whereby new men can be mentored and prepared eventually
to step into the role of Secretary. Assistant Secretaries are authorized by Lodges
to assist elected Secretaries in the performance of all their duties. In addition,
assistant secretaries are allowed to be compensated by Lodges for their services
as Lodges may direct. This resolution will reduce the impetus that Lodges would
otherwise have to identify such Assistant Secretaries.
This resolution is not consistent with the policy and general purposes of
this Grand Lodge.
Resolution NOT ADOPTED.
Resolution No. 09-08
Entitles Secretary and Inspector to a Vote at Grand Lodge
The proponents of this Resolution seek to allow Lodge Secretaries and District
Inspectors a separate vote at the Annual Communication of the Grand Lodge of
California.
To the Most Worshipful Grand Lodge, F.& A.M. of California:
WHEREAS, Lodge Secretaries and District Inspectors are generally
very knowledgeable about Lodge activities, as well as its finances, typically for
multiple years, and are, therefore, uniquely suited to an understanding of how
legislation will affect their respective Lodges; and
WHEREAS, Lodge Secretaries and District Inspectors are more likely
to attend the Annual Communication of the Grand Lodge if they had an ownership
in the voting process; and
WHEREAS, allowing Lodge Secretaries and District Inspectors to
have a vote at Grand Lodge would encourage timely communication of balloting
results to their respective members; and
WHEREAS, allowing approximately 339 Lodge Secretaries and 101
District Inspectors a vote would not jeopardize current voting block of 1,356
Lodge delegates (Master, Wardens and Past Master votes); and
WHEREAS, allowing Lodge Secretaries and District Inspectors to join

214

Proceedings of the

the voting team at Grand Lodge would ensure that the face of Masonry at the
Lodge level is always represented on the floor of Grand Lodge.
NOW, THEREFORE, BE IT RESOLVED, that Section 403.075 of
the California Masonic Code be amended as marked:
§403.075. VOTES.
Except as provided in Sections 403.080 and 403.085 of this Code, the following
shall each be entitled to one vote only:
A.

Elective and appointive Grand Lodge Officers except the Grand Tiler
and the Assistant Grand Tiler;

B.

Past Grand Officers;

C.

Inspectors;

D.

Masters, Senior Wardens, and Junior Wardens and Secretaries of
chartered Lodges; and

D.E.

Past Masters collectively of each chartered Lodge.

If neither the Master nor either of the Wardens nor the Secretary of a Lodge has
registered, the elected representative of the Lodge may cast the votes of Master,
and Wardens and Secretary of the Lodge.
BE IT FURTHER RESOLVED, that Section 403.080 of the California
Masonic Code be amended as marked:
§403.080. CUMULATIVE VOTING.
If one or more of the Master, and Wardens and Secretary of a Lodge has not
registered, or has registered and is not present, the senior such officer registered,
in addition to his own vote, may also cast the vote of the absentee or absentees. If
an incumbent officer of Grand Lodge, or a Past Grand Officer or an Inspector is
also a Master, Warden, Secretary or elected representative of a Lodge, he may cast
all votes to which such positions entitle him, but he shall not be entitled to cast
the vote afforded the Past Masters of a Lodge. If the elected representative is a
Past Master but not an incumbent officer of Grand Lodge, or a Past Grand Officer
or an Inspector, he may also cast the vote afforded the Past Masters of a Lodge.

Grand Lodge F. & A.M. of California

215

Report of the Committee on Policy and General Purposes:
The proponents of Resolution No. 09-08, seek to allow Lodge Secretaries
and District Inspectors a separate vote at the annual communication of the Grand
Lodge.
Article 20 of the Constitution of the Grand Lodge of California defines
the members as all the installed Grand Lodge Officers, all the Past Grand Officers,
all Past Masters of this jurisdiction, and the current Masters and Wardens of the
Lodges.
With reference to the portion of the resolution pertaining to the District
Inspectors, it should be noted that all Inspectors are Past Masters and therefore
are members of the Grand Lodge and share a vote along with the Past Masters of
their respective Lodges. However, Inspectors in recent years have assumed ever
increasing responsibilities as the Personal Representative of the Grand Master in
their respective Districts where they typically serve for several years and where
they are very well informed on issues pertaining to their respective Lodges. As
this committee reported last year, giving a vote to Inspectors is consistent with
our policy.
Although many Lodge Secretaries are Past Masters, such is not a
requirement to serve as Lodge Secretary and in fact, some Lodge Secretaries are
not Past Masters. To give Lodge Secretaries a vote at the annual communication
would be to grant a vote to those Lodge Secretaries who are not Past Masters and
who, by definition, are not members of the Grand Lodge.
The Committee on Policy and General Purposes believes that this portion
of Resolution 09-08, and thus the resolution as a whole, is not consistent with the
policies and general purposes of the Grand Lodge.
Resolution NOT ADOPTED.

§1200.035. ADDITIONAL FINANCIAL SUPPORT.
A Masonic Organization may solicit and receive financial support for its fraternal
operations and charitable activities from the following additional sources:
A.

A Masonic Organization may hold periodic activities to which tickets
are sold to its members and their families and friends, as well as to the
general public, when done within the bounds of propriety and when fair
prices are charged. No tickets to such an activity may be sold to the
general public nor may there be any other public solicitation of support
for such an activity. The sponsor shall disclose that any profits from
the activity will be used for the sponsor’s fraternal and not charitable
purposes, if such is the case. In the event that such disclosure is not
given, through inadvertence or otherwise, all proceeds from the activity
shall be given to a recognized Masonic charity;

any such words may not be used in connection with the activity
or any publicity associated with it;
2.

§1200.050. GAMBLING.
Neither a Masonic Organization nor any of its members shall, directly or indirectly,
give or attempt to give the aid or countenance of Masonry to, or participate in, the
conduct or preparation of any raffle, lottery or other form of gambling, conducted
in the name of, or directly or indirectly for the monetary benefit of, any Lodge or
of any Masonic Organization. A Masonic Organization shall not accept any of the
proceeds or profits of any raffle, lottery or other gambling enterprise. Prizes may
be awarded if not purchased from monies collected as admission fees and if no
separate charge is made for the chance to win.
The foregoing notwithstanding, Masonic Organizations and their members may
conduct raffles, lotteries and bingo games for the benefit of their charities and for

A.
cConducted strictly in accordance with all applicable federal, state and

and Conducted strictly in accordance with the provisions hereunder

BE IT FURTHER RESOLVED, that Section 1200.055 of the California
Masonic Code which now reads as follows be repealed:
§1200.055. BINGO.
A Masonic Organization may sponsor a bingo game, if the sponsor observes the
following rules:
A.

Attendance at the event is limited to the sponsor’s members, their
families and friends;

B.

There are no signs or other advertising visible to the general public
referring to the game;

C.

No donation, admission fee, meal purchase or other charge is accepted or
required for the opportunity to participate; and

D.

Prizes of minimal value may be awarded if purchased by the sponsor or
donated.

Report of the Committee on Policy and General Purposes:
This proposed legislation will make fundamental changes in the
California Masonic Code pertaining to fund raising by Masonic Organizations.
The proponents seek to change Section 1200.010 to more closely match the
intent of Section 1300.010 that pertains to Masonic Youth Orders. MasonicYouth
Orders are structurally different from other Masonic organizations, and allowing
Masonic Organizations to operate under the same rules as the Masonic Youth
Orders is inconsistent with our established policy.
Much of the commentary in Section 1200.050 has been deleted, leaving
little guidance for Masonic Organizations in the raising of funds, and will allow
gambling as a source of fraternal income for these organizations. Income from
gambling by Masonic Organizations for fraternal operations is not consistent with
the policy and general purposes of this Grand Lodge.

Grand Lodge F. & A.M. of California

219

While the committee is not in a position to offer an opinion on the tax
consequences of this proposal as it pertains to non-profit organizations, the risks
of soliciting the public for financial support for non-charitable fraternal activities
should be carefully considered.
For these reasons the committee believes that this proposal is not
consistent with the policy and general purposes of Grand Lodge.
Resolution CARRIED OVER.

§1200.050. GAMBLING.

proceeds or profits of any raffle, lottery or other gambling enterprise. Prizes may

separate charge is made for the chance to win.

§1200.055. BINGO.

1200.050, if the sponsor observes the following rules:

222

Proceedings of the

B.

There are no signs or other advertising visible to the general public
referring to the game;

No donation, admission fee, meal purchase or other charge is accepted or
required for the opportunity to participate; and

D.

Prizes of minimal value may be awarded if purchased by the sponsor or
donated.

Report of the Committee on Policy and General Purposes:
Prior to 1995, and dating back at least as far as 1936, the policy of
Grand Lodge had been to prohibit gambling in any form, without exception. This
prohibition applied regardless of the intended purpose of the activity, whether to
raise funds for charities, to pay operating expenses, or merely as a social activity.
It included bingo, raffles, door prizes, dice and card games – essentially anything
in which money was collected and prizes awarded. It applied to both Craft
Lodges and Concordant and Appendant bodies.
Beginning in 1970, a series of resolutions were proposed to change
this policy but none were adopted. The Policy and General Purposes committee
reported on each such occasion that Masonry should have no association with
gambling of any type.
The first modification to this policy occurred in 1995, when bingo for
purely social purposes was allowed, provided no money was involved. If prizes
were awarded, they must be donated by the sponsor and of minimal value.
The next modification occurred in 2006, when Grand Lodge approved
Grand Master Doan’s Recommendation 2005-02, which permitted Concordant
and Appendant Masonic Organizations to conduct raffles for the benefit of their
charities and for Masonic Youth Orders to be able to conduct raffles for their
charities and special needs, provided such activities did not conflict with Federal,
State, and Local laws. The recommendation did not permit gambling of any type
other than raffles, nor did it allow the proceeds from such activities to be used for
operating expenses.
Resolution 09-10 would go even further and allow only the Shrine to
conduct raffles, lotteries, and bingo with the proceeds being used for operating
expenses as well as charities. It would permit such events to be offered to the
general public. It would not apply to Craft Lodges or any other Appendant and
Concordant bodies.
As stated in our report on Resolution No. 09-09, this committee
is concerned about using money from gambling for fraternal operations of
Masonic Organizations, the failure to recognize the difference between Masonic
Organizations and Masonic Youth Orders, and the potential tax consequences of
this legislation.

Grand Lodge F. & A.M. of California

223

The temptation to seek an easy way to raise funds in difficult times is
forever present, but the strict Masonic Laws regarding gambling which were
framed by our forefathers have served well to protect our reputation from scandal,
legal entanglements, and negative community opinions of our Fraternity.
This resolution is not consistent with the policy and general purposes of
this Grand Lodge.
Resolution NOT ADOPTED.

Proceedings of the
Board Meetings

Meetings of the full Board were held in the months of November,
February and June, alternating between the Union City and Covina campuses.
In the months in which a Board meeting was not scheduled, meetings of the key
committees of the Board were held. In addition the Board held its annual two-day

Acacia Creek Board of Directors
By Michael D. Neben, President
It has been an eventful year for Acacia Creek-Union City and Acacia
Creek-Covina, filled with reasons to celebrate and darker times when very difficult
decisions had to be made.
Looming over the entire year was the unprecedented economic downturn
that hit our nation in the Fall of 2008. We were confronted by a triple challenge:
the bursting of the real estate market, the downward spiral of the stock market,
and the worst crisis in the credit market that this nation had seen in decades.
Worse: the three challenges were entwined, each reinforcing the vulnerability of
the others.
ACACIA CREEK – UNION CITY
•

Cornerstone Ceremony. On August 22, 2008, the Cornerstone Ceremony
for Acacia Creek kicked off its vertical construction phase. A cornerstone
of Jerusalem Marble, with a bronze face containing details of the ceremony,
was placed in the traditional manner, with Grand Master Hopper squaring the
stone, Deputy Grand Master Adamson leveling it and Senior Grand Warden
Nagel assuring that it was plumb. A time capsule sealed earlier in the day
contained AC’s working drawings, legal documents, and other pertinent
historical items. Acacia Creek Founders (depositors) and their families,
members interested in future residence, and Masonic Homes residents and
staff were invited. Officers of the Grand Lodge, the Masonic Homes Board
of Trustees, the Acacia Creek Board of Directors, and local dignitaries were
in attendance.

•

Construction. The construction of AC-Union City, started the prior
year, continued largely on schedule the past year. The Site Development
Committee helped oversee an able team of staff and project consultants to
manage the construction. We are projecting that AC-Union City will come in
at budget, with an opening in late January, 2010, with thanks in no small part
to the dedicated staff and consultants who aggressively oversee the minutest
details of this project every day. A happy development occurred during the
year: through the generosity of Franklin Lodge, AC-Union City came into

Grand Lodge F. & A.M. of California

229

possession of a series of stained glass windows that were a part of the Lodge
for many years. The windows have been removed from the Franklin Lodge
building and are undergoing minor repairs. Upon completion of the Acacia
Creek building at Union City, these windows will be displayed as artworks
in key locations of the public areas to serve as a reminder of AC’s heritage.
•

Sales and Marketing. Sales in every continuing care retirement community
(CCRC) depends on both the real estate and stock market since nearly all
CCRC residents fund their entry fee from the sale of their homes and pay
ongoing monthly fees from their other assets. A recent survey conducted
by Ziegler and Associates, the largest investment banker for senior housing
communities, indicated that well over 80% of start-up CCRCs have been
negatively affected by the economy. Acacia Creek was no exception. The
promising rate of sales in 2007 and early 2008 began to slow in the Fall of
2008 when the economic downturn began and the slow pace of sales has
continued since then. 2009 has been a difficult year for sales. In August,
2009, the Acacia Creek Board approved $2 million for a “marketing toolbox”
of programs designed to help with Founder retention, and continues to look
for other strategies and methods to help marketing staff enhance sales.

•

Operational Ramp-up. Management has spent the past six months preparing
for opening in January, 2010. Lydia Hertzler was hired as Administrator
of Acacia Creek in July, 2009, and she is reviewing programs and staffing
assumptions, preparing the operating budget, revising hiring and training
schedules, and other critical ramp-up activities.

ACACIA CREEK-COVINA
As we approached the Fall of 2008, Acacia Creek-Covina had achieved
all of the prerequisite milestones committed to by the Board and described in the
Acacia Creek Report for the 2008 Grand Lodge Proceedings. That is to say, ACCovina had California Department of Social Services approvals; reached 70%
presales (10% deposits); completed architectural design and received all local
entitlements; and secured an initial guaranteed maximum price from the contractor.
Acacia Creek-Covina was nearing its financing date when the economic downturn
began. The credit market almost immediately began to freeze up. Banks which
had previously indicated interest in participating backtracked; and even Banks
with which we had contractual arrangements began to be restive.
•

Decision to Suspend. When it became clear by January, 2009 that Acacia
Creek-Covina would not be able to obtain the necessary financing on terms
it found acceptable nor was the prospect of doing so imminent, the difficult
decision was made to suspend the project while the Board monitored the
economic landscape. In making the decision, the Board was motivated by
its duty to be candid and forthcoming to AC-Covina’s depositors. Depositors

230

Proceedings of the
gave their money in good faith, and deserved to be treated in the same manner.
Depositors were individually notified by Board members, with the assistance
of members of the Masonic Homes Board. All depositors were grateful for
the notice; many thanked us for being prudent.

•

Decision to Close. The AC-Covina Board continued to monitor the economic
situation over the months and consulted with the Grand Treasurer and the
Grand Lodge Investment Committee. It became evident by the Summer that
none of the three elements of the downturn—the real estate, equity and credit
markets—showed any signs of sufficient recovery in the near future and
that all three needed to recover for AC-Covina to be viable. The consensus
opinion was that it would take some years to recover what had been lost.
Thus, the Board made the painful but necessary decision to close the project.
Again, the Board was largely motivated by its obligation to depositors. If it
honestly felt that financing for AC-Covina was years in the future, it could
not allow depositors to entertain any false hopes. The decision of the Acacia
Creek Board was brought before the Board of Masonic Homes of California,
which created the organization and was its sole member, and the MHC Board
concurred with the decision.

The decision to close Acacia Creek-Covina was painful to members of
the Board, who had given generously of their time and energy to a project they
believed in fully. Despite the personal pain, they did so willingly because they
understood their fiduciary responsibility was first and foremost to their brethren,
and that the reality of the economic downturn could not be denied.
The Acacia Creek Board is extremely indebted to those members of
the fraternity who made an early commitment to become residents of our Union
City campus. These depositors saw the vision just as the Board did and made
life changing decisions, many before they ever saw the physical building taking
shape. Over the past year, they have created a new bond amongst themselves and
are eagerly awaiting the opportunity to move in to their new homes in January of
2010.
In many ways, the decision to close Acacia Creek-Covina revealed more
about the dedication and commitment of the members of the Acacia Creek Board
and to Masonic values than if AC-Covina had proceeded in more normal fashion
and in more normal times. It is therefore with pride and gratitude that I recognize
the members of the Board of Directors of Acacia Creek as:
Michael D. Neben, President
Mark N. Gibson, Vice President
Fred L. Avery

Loyal L. Stark

George L. Geanoulis
Frank Loui
Ara Maloyan

President’s Report
By David R. Doan, PGM
This has been a very eventful year for the Masonic Homes of California.
The financial crisis that has impacted so much of society, including many of our
members, impacted the Masonic Homes as well. I am pleased to report to you that
the Homes has weathered this financial storm.
While we saw a reduction in our investments and income stream, the
Board was able to maintain a balanced budget while maintaining our high standard
of care for our residents. The Homes maintain a very conservative posture in our
investments and in our budgeting. The Board also maintains a reserve fund to
smooth out the unpredictable major cost or revenue fluctuations, such as we have
seen this year. The Board asked management to project the long term effects of
the market downturn on the Homes’ finances. The Board reviewed the projections
and approved a contingency plan that will serve as the blueprint of how the Homes
will weather the financial storm and its after effects over the next five to six years.
In 2007, the Homes received a credit rating of “AA” from Fitch and
Standard & Poor’s rating agencies. In July of this year the Homes rating was
reviewed by Fitch in light of the financial downturn. Fitch reaffirmed its “AA”
rating for Masonic Homes, citing among other things, its financial position,
despite the general deterioration of the stock market, its stable management, and
its business judgment.
The Board is cautiously optimistic that we have weathered the current
financial storm. The Board continues to look for ways to increase the income
of the Homes. In addition to our fund raising efforts and short term projects to
reduce costs and improve efficiencies, the Board has undertaken several long term
projects that will generate future income to the Homes.
As you know the Homes created Acacia Creek, a separate nonprofit
corporation to develop unused Homes property. In a few years when the project
is fully operational the Homes will receive income in the form of lease payments
that will help to fund the charitable mission of the Homes. There will be a separate
report from the President of Acacia Creek on its activities.
The Board has also undertaken a project to develop the approximately 63
acres below the Union City Home and northward along Mission Blvd to Whipple
Avenue. The first goal remains voter approval at the November 2010 general
election; because of a previous initiative passed by voters, our flatlands were
included in the hillside ordinance prohibiting any use other than agricultural uses.
Our goal is to get the restrictions meant for the hillside removed from the flatlands.
The outreach campaign is in full swing. Entitlement work is on schedule; we
are entering the environmental impact report phase, which we have undertaken
voluntarily as a measure of our good faith with the electorate.

232

Proceedings of the

The design concept remains a work in progress but the original overall
idea of a mixed-use project with residential and retail remains viable, including
the possibility that some of the residential units would be seniors housing of some
kind. We enjoy the support of Union City and many community leaders and we
are hopeful that with voter approval in the next ten years the flatlands will begin
to generate income for the Homes.
The Masonic Homes, for the period of July 1, 2008 to June 30, 2009,
received 64 applications and admitted 45 new residents to both campuses. As of
June 30, Union City had 258 residents, including the 16 residents in the Traditions
Special Care Unit. Covina had 78 residents.
The Masonic Outreach Services program continues to grow and is an
effective program in assisting our eligible Brethren and their families or widows.
This program provides the necessary support, financial, housing and/or care
management assistant to clients who are in need of assistance to remain in their
present community or residence. As you might imagine, many of our elderly
brethren, their wives and widows have been hard hit by recent economic events.
As a result, we are serving more members through our MOS program than ever
before in our history. This year, approximately 185 members received direct
financial assistance through our MOS program; another 200 received information
and referral or ongoing case management services.
I would like to share a story of how we have used your charity to help
our Brothers. A Brother, who is 80 years old, having exhausted his savings and
unable to pay his rent, found himself homeless and spent 5 days sleeping in his
car. His lodge intervened, and called the Homes for help. He was put on a fast
track for MOS care. The lodge provided Safeway cards so that he could eat and
helped him find a temporary place to live. MOS was approved and staff was able
to find him a permanent place to live, moved him in and now helps with expenses
each month. Lodge members visit regularly and our Brother is in a stable and
healthy environment with just a little extra money from the Homes for his rent
and food every month. Just a little bit of money and a lot of Brotherly love goes
a long way.
The Board annually conducts satisfaction surveys of our residents. The
annual satisfaction survey of residents continued to indicate very high satisfaction
rates. Residents, at all three levels of care, rated overall satisfaction well above
normative benchmark and in the top tenth percentile of all facilities.
As part of our continuing effort to improve operations and to validate
the quality of our services, the Board has pursued accreditation from a recognized
accreditation organization. This year after a rigorous risk management
assessment, interview of key management and on-site review and observation of
our operations at both campuses the Board was pleased to receive accreditation
from Praesidium, a recognized accreditation organization.

Grand Lodge F. & A.M. of California

233

For more than a century, the Masonic Homes of California has
provided relief to children and families in the name of Freemasonry. Over the
years our services have evolved to address the changing needs of our fraternity
and society: from 1898, when we opened the Masonic Home for widows and
orphans; to 1997, when the fraternity voted to accept children with no Masonic
affiliation to the residential program for children at Covina. While our services
have changed, our commitment to our founding values has remained steadfast.
Different times require different responses. Residential Children programs pose
inherent risks to any organization and are an outdated model of care. They are
also limited in reach, as our program served just 15 children from Los Angeles
Country, only three of whom have a Masonic connection.
The Masonic Homes of California has the potential, and the fraternal
obligation, to do much more. Accordingly, the difficult decision was made to
transition out of residential services for children and into a Masonic Outreach
Service model for children. The last day of operations for the residential
program was July 31, 2009.
The decision was made after a comprehensive and thorough review
of our facilities; the needs of our children and families and how to best meet
them; the changing regulatory environment; and the changing demographics
and strategic needs of our fraternity.
We spent months planning for the transition and consulted with clinical
experts on how to best manage it. All the children in our residential program
went through a thorough and careful discharge process and each family was
assigned a care manager to help with their transition. Each of the children has
been placed in an appropriate environment based on their individual needs. As
with all graduates, we will follow these children and their families to support
their well-being.
The Homes continues to provide scholarships to the alumni of our
residential program. We are currently providing scholarships to 22 former
residents. These impressive young adults are attending four-year universities
and colleges; two-year junior colleges; and a variety of vocational programs. We
closely monitor their academic progress through our Family Resource Center.
We are forming a task force comprised of Trustees, Homes executive
staff, Grand Lodge staff and Masonic members to come up with a plan to put the
former children’s houses into service for our fraternity. We will report on our
progress and plans in future issues of The Leader and the California Freemason.
As part of the strategic shift of our organization, we are expanding the
services of the Family Resource Center to better meet the needs of our fraternal
family with a focus on children. Within the first month of this transition, we
have already doubled the number of Masonic children we are serving.

234

Proceedings of the

Two of our brothers in Northern California have children who are
experiencing great emotional difficulties; we have helped assess their issues
and to find the right treatment programs for them. One of our brothers in San
Diego is involved in a very difficult divorce and we are helping him provide the
appropriate resources to help his nine-year old son cope with stress. One of our
deceased brothers in Southern California has a grandchild, with no stable home,
who we are helping to support in a boarding school; and two of our brothers’
grandchildren are receiving ongoing care management, financial and therapeutic
support. We are no longer bound by the limits of the types of care we could
provide in a residential program or by how far our services were from a Masonic
family in need. We are now able to help Masonic Children all across California.
Through the generous efforts and guiding vision of Mrs. Judi Hopper,
promoting children’s literacy is a key component of our Family Resource Center.
We are working to have books in the hands of our Masonic children and promoting
literacy in the name of Freemasonry. Our activities include sponsoring reading
contests, scholarships for books, an “Ask-the-Librarian” service for parents
interested in recommendations, essay contests and book give-a-ways to lowincome children.
Through the Family Resource Center, we are also developing programs
and services to meet the needs of our fraternal family across the age spectrum,
with a particular focus on those families hard-hit by the recent economic downturn.
To that end, we are facilitating six Masonic Resource Fairs across the
state to bring together resources and supportive services to our brothers who have
lost their job or may be struggling with mortgage or other financial issues. We
scheduled fairs in:
Pleasanton
Anaheim
Fresno

Sacramento
Redding
San Diego

Turn-out has been strong and we continue to research options to find
services and information for our brothers in need.
The Homes work with children has changed over the years from just
providing room and board to working with children that have complex emotional
and behavior issues. The Homes staff has been frustrated on more than one
occasion when it has taken multiple visits to different care providers for testing
and evaluation to determine all of a child’s issues that need to be addressed. We
have looked at the reasons for a process that sometimes feels like trial and error.
We have reached the conclusion that it is because most children had access to only
one of the three disciplines that work with children.

Grand Lodge F. & A.M. of California

235

Most children today are seen by either a psychologist, psychiatrist or
a social worker, each of whom has a different approach to testing and treating a
child. We worked with experts from the three fields and a world class expert on
testing and came up with a process that we believe is a significant improvement.
We used this approach in determining the best course of treatment for one of
our Masonic children in need. By combining all three approaches into one
comprehensive evaluation process we got a detailed treatment plan that used
all of the tools available to develop a treatment plan for the child. The child is
responding well to the treatment plan and his family has seen the difference. The
child has stated that for the first time he feels that people understand him and that
someone really cares about him. We helped save a child from what could have
been a very tragic future.
The Masonic Homes recently completed extensive research into the
needs of our fraternal family and our communities and after talking with national
and international experts we discovered the following:
•

An estimated 4,700 of our members have children or grandchildren
struggling with learning disorders; 3,500 of them are going undiagnosed
and untreated.

•

An estimated 4,700 of our members have children or grandchildren
struggling with emotional or behavioral issues; 3,900 of them are going
undiagnosed and untreated.

•

Children undiagnosed and untreated for these disorders have significantly
higher rates of drop out, drug and alcohol abuse, incarceration and teen
pregnancy.

•

Treatment is possible. We can and must prevent such tragic loss of
potential.

As a result the Board completed a comprehensive strategic and business
plan to open a new Masonic Center for Children and Families that will use this
comprehensive model. The child will go through as much testing and assessment
as is warranted and the family will then meet with the treatment team, which
will include psychologists, psychiatrists and social workers. Together they will
arrive at a plan that addresses the child’s and the families’ needs that will include
recommendations on treatment, medication (if appropriate) and educational
environment. A treatment manager will help implement that plan in the home
community.
The Homes are committed to the Masonic Center for Children and
Families providing world-class services, research and becoming a leader in the
field. We are contracting with the best providers in the country and are exploring
partnerships with leading universities, including Yale and Baylor. This project is
being designed to be evaluated with the intent for a published research paper on
the results.

236

Proceedings of the

We are planning to open our doors in November 2010. We will provide
regular updates to you on our progress. In the meantime, if you or one of our
brothers knows of a Masonic family struggling with such issues, please contact
our Family Resource Center. Additional information and the contact info on this
program are available at the Homes Booth downstairs.
This Annual Communication marks the end of nine years of service for
two members of the Board of Trustees. Worshipful George Geanoulis, a Past

ever increasing areas of responsibility, with Brother Yanes having service this
year as the Board’s Vice President. The Board and I thank them for their service
and wish them well.
On behalf of the entire Board of Trustees, we thank the Grand Master
and Brethren for the opportunity to be of service to the craft. The Masonic Homes
of California is a great charitable organization and you can be proud of the work
that it does in the name of Freemasonry.

Report of the Executive Vice President
MANAGEMENT CHANGES
In June, 2009, we said thanks and goodbye to Rob Fallon, Executive
Director of Senior Services, who had been with the organization since April,
2001, first as Executive Director of the Union City campus and then as Executive
Director overseeing both campuses’ senior divisions. Rob and his wife Jennifer
had been mulling a move back to their native Boston, and with their children
nearing school age, they thought now was the time to make the move. Rob
helped to improve care and services to residents by introducing raised standards
of performance within an atmosphere of openness and welcome to both residents
and employees. We wish Rob and his family well.
In July, 2009, we welcomed Lydia Hertzler, Administrator of Acacia
Creek-Union City. Lydia came to us with a passion for serving seniors,
enthusiasm for her work, and a wealth of experience. Her resume was particularly
strong in start-ups, having opened four different facilities during her career. That
experience will be invaluable as we gear up for opening in January, 2010 and the
inevitable hiccups that new facilities encounter.
ACACIA CREEK
Acacia Creek presented us with its share of challenges, as detailed under
the Acacia Creek report authored by Board President Mike Neben. The decision
to suspend and eventually close down AC-Covina was difficult and painful, but it
was a sound decision, given what happened in the credit marketplace as a result
of the general economic turmoil. Sales have been a challenge for AC-Union City,
again because of the general economic crisis. We cannot discount the challenges,
but we also look forward to our opening in late January with enthusiasm. We have
an opportunity to serve the Masonic family in a new way, and we expect to do so
as a stellar reflection of Masonic values.
CHILDREN’S SERVICES
Please see the report of Steffani Kizziar, Executive Director of Children’s
and Outreach Services, for details regarding the transition of children’s services
from residential homes to an outreach program under the Family Resource Center.
As difficult as this decision was, we are finding confirmation during the early
stages of FRC that we are serving Masonic children and families in a way more
in sync with their needs today. We expect this program to grow stronger in the
coming year.

238

Proceedings of the
MASONIC CENTER FOR CHILDREN & FAMILIES

Also in her report, Steffani Kizziar describes a new program approved
by the Board of Trustees in August, 2009 for the Masonic Center for Children
and Families, which we expect to become a center of excellence in testing for and
therapeutic treatment of difficult behavioral and psychological conditions. Work
has already started with a targeting opening in November, 2010.
FLATLANDS PROJECT
The flatlands project, previously called the frontage property project,
remains one of our key strategic initiatives. John Howl, Vice President of
Strategic Development, has provided able leadership to the process of obtaining,
first and foremost, voter approval in November, 2010 to develop the property and
second, the necessary entitlements. For more details, please see the report of the
Strategic Planning Committee of the Board of Trustees, authored by Chairman
Mark Gibson.
MANAGEMENT INCENTIVE PROGRAM
The MIP was redesigned for the coming year. In recognition of economic
conditions and the duty to always search for ways to improve accountability, the
2009-10 MIP features:
•

redefined SMART goals that are tied to the key duties of managers
(budget, employee safety, satisfaction survey participation) in addition
to appropriate strategic goals;

•

base salary adjustment at-risk pending meeting of goals; and

•

payment partly in base salary adjustment and partly in a lump-sum.

We expect this redesign to tighten accountability by clarifying
expectations at every level of management and tying base-salary increases to
achievement of goals.
Respectfully submitted,
Melvin Matsumoto
