Report of the Associate Executive Director/Administrator

Masonic Home at Union City

It is an honor and privilege to submit the annual report on behalf of the
residents and staff of the Masonic Home in Union City.
This year the work of staff has been nationally recognized for two
significant achievements. Among them was working with Covina and Union City
staff to attain national certification for abuse prevention by Praesidium. Masonic
Homes of California has been awarded Accreditation™ by Praesidium, the national
leader in abuse risk management. Praesidium Accreditation is a prestigious
honor that publicly demonstrates to residents and families that Masonic Homes
of California has worked to achieve the highest industry standards in abuse
prevention.
To attain accreditation, Masonic Homes of California underwent a
rigorous risk assessment to review and scrutinize eight primary operational
areas within their business including: policies, screening and selection, training,
monitoring and supervision, consumer participation, feedback systems, responding
and administrative systems. Their assessment results were benchmarked against
The Safety Equation™—800 best practice standards and empirically based data
collection methods to assess the risk of abuse in an organization. Masonic Homes
of California will be accredited for three years, and during this time will uphold
fundamental organizational values and stringent safety practices that demonstrate
their commitment to protecting those in their care from abuse.
Praesidium Accreditation provides solid proof of sound risk management
practices and the honor distinguishes Masonic Homes of California from others
in the Senior Care Services. We are pleased to send the message that we will
not compromise the safety of those in our care—for any reason. As an entire
organization, we are dedicated and committed to proactively keeping those in our
care safe.
The second recognition came when the Masonic Home at Union City
was selected by Mather LifeWays Institute on Aging (MLIA) as first-place winner
of the Promising Practices Award, which recognizes the retirement community’s
organizational disaster readiness program.
The Promising Practices Award honors the Masonic Home for the
outstanding steps taken to improve organizational readiness for a disaster. The
Union City campus has developed a comprehensive approach to planning for
manmade or natural disasters in order to better serve the unique needs of their
older adult population.

240

Proceedings of the

This year there were some new beginnings as well as a farewell to Dr.
Sheila Raumer. Dr. Raumer had been the Medical Director for seven years. This
September she and her husband relocated to Germany. We were pleased to have
Dr. Bala Annadurai accept the position of Medical Director. Dr. Annadurai has
been a physician at Masonic Home of California, Union City for several years,
and knows many of our residents quite well. In February, Dr. Benjamin Chew
replaced Dr. Zuri Barniv as the Homes on-site dentist. He has been well-received
by the residents. Dr. Rick Smith was designated as the physician who will see
Masonic Homes Kaiser residents in the SNF area. Dr. Smith is board certified
in internal medicine, as well as geriatric medicine. This allows the SNF Kaiser
residents to be seen on campus instead of having to go to outside appointments.
A key achievement during this year has been the renovation of Lorber,
which began January 5, 2009. The upgrades include expansion of the dining room,
upgrade of the nurse call system and the creation of a living room area at the third
floor entrance. Other improvements are the lighting in the hallways, flooring in
resident rooms and halls, and reducing the size of the nurse station. The piece de
resistance is the completely renovated bathing area. Other changes include paint,
furniture and fixture replacement and relocation of offices to the interior walls that
allows resident spaces to have access to the exterior windows.
The Return to Home Act has enabled us to bring the residents who have
Health Maintenance Organization (HMO) insurance coverage back to the Union
City campus following an Acute Hospital stay for Rehabilitation and nursing
care. This is opposed to an admission to an outside Skilled Nursing Facility. The
Masonic home is able to negotiate with the local HMO’s on an individual case
basis.
A new wheelchair bus arrived in December and is in regular use for
outings with the Lorber residents. The ride is smooth and all residents who have
ridden in the bus are pleased with the purchase.
A new resident monitoring system was initiated in the independent
section of the Home. This system allows non-intrusive, computerized, compliance
with Department of Social Service Title 22 requirement for daily check-in of all
residents in the Independent and Assisted Living areas.
There are a variety of opportunities for resident involvement and activity
on the campus. Of significance this year has been the creation of a parking
committee. Parking has been a campus wide issue that became more urgent with
the construction occurring on campus. To address the concerns, a resident and staff
committee was created. The committee has been very active in developing and
implementing a plan of action, with management approval. The results include
assigned parking for residents, event parking designated to the outlying parking
areas and creation of approximately 10 more spaces to alleviate staff parking
needs. A six person shuttle, the people-mover, was purchased to assist staff and

Grand Lodge F. & A.M. of California

241

residents with transportation to and from the outlying parking lots. The shuttle
schedule is organized and managed by the committee. Residents are the volunteer
drivers. The program has alleviated a significant piece of the need for parking.
The gardening group has grown larger as many of the newer residents
have expressed a desire to be involved with the ever-expanding Masonic Homes
garden. The North Forty has been expanded to a full acre of land. The garden is
producing fruits and vegetables during the summer and winter. The residents look
forward to the announcement in the dining room that the tomatoes or beans or
corn being served that day are Masonic Home vegetables ‘freshly harvested that
morning’ from the garden. This adds depth to the dining experience.
A group of residents asked to explore options for a cooking area to
replace the loss of a full kitchen from their prior homes. In response, the kitchen
in second Adams was set up with basic equipment. This area is being used on a
regular basis by residents for cooking and baking. The residents express great
pleasure to be able to continue cooking as they did at home. Some of them are able
to use the produce they have grown in the garden off second Adams.
The resident woodshop has moved to the basement of Wollenberg. The
woodshop is now easily accessible and in a centralized location between the Home
and Acacia Creek. It is anticipated that some of the Acacia Creek residents will
want to participate in the woodshop and now the shop will be ready to share the
space. The Facilities Maintenance shop has been moved to the previous location
of the residents and Maintenance wood shops. The residents are happy with the
new location.
In recent years it was noted that the Chapel of the Chimes Masonic
Cemetery needed to be upgraded. Work began in the late fall with grooming
and sod replacement. The rotation of markers has been accomplished. Currently
we are working with Art Monuments regarding placement of the 100 markers
recently purchased.
A major project of upgrading the phone system has been completed on
the Union City campus. The system will eventually connect Grand Lodge, Union
City and Covina campuses onto one system.

252

Proceedings of the

Report of the Executive Director
of Children’s and Masonic Outreach Services
CHILDREN’S SERVICES
In this fiscal year, the Board of Trustees and Executive Management
made the difficult decision to transition out of residential services for children and
the last day of operations was July 31st, 2009.
At the time of our closing, our residential program served 21 children;
only three of whom had any Masonic affiliation, with the rest coming from the
immediate Los Angeles County. The decision was reached after a comprehensive
and thorough review of our facilities; the needs of our Masonic children and
families; the changing regulatory environment; and the changing demographics
and strategic needs of our fraternity.
We planned carefully for the transition in close consultation with clinical
experts. All the children went through a thorough and careful discharge process
and each family was assigned a care manager to help with their transition. As with
all graduates, we will follow these children and their families closely and support
their well-being. The program employees were treated with equal consideration.
They received severance pay, job search assistance, and housing relocation as
appropriate.
We are pleased to announce that this year twenty-one alumni were
awarded scholarships to pursue higher education in community colleges, state
universities and trade schools. We have a social worker dedicated to following
these young adults and ensuring their academic success.
We have formed a task force comprised of Trustees, Masonic Homes
executive staff, Grand Lodge staff and Masonic members to develop a plan to
put the former children’s houses into service for our fraternity. Our strategic plan
will be completed in the coming year and our results will be announced in the
California Freemason.
MASONIC FAMILY RESOURCE CENTER
As part of the strategic shift of our organization, we are expanding the
services of the Masonic Family Resource Center, which is located on our Covina
campus in Southern California, to better meet the needs of our fraternal family.
We offer case management services that are broad, flexible, and able to serve
families in their own communities. Our support includes identifying resources
for families struggling with today’s complex issues, such as the impact of divorce,
the stresses of a special needs child, and other significant life challenges. Less
than one month into our transition, we have already doubled the number of
Masonic children we are serving.

Grand Lodge F. & A.M. of California

253

Through the Masonic Family Resource Center, we are also developing
programs and services to meet the needs of our fraternal family hard-hit by the
recent economic down-turn. To that end, we are conducting six Masonic Resource
Fairs across the state to bring together resources and supportive services to our
brothers who have lost their job or may be struggling with mortgage or other
financial issues. We have fairs scheduled in:
• Pleasanton
• Anaheim
• Fresno
• Sacramento
• Redding
• San Diego
Turn-out has been strong and we continue to research options to help our
brothers in need.
Through the generous efforts and guiding vision of Mrs. Judi Hopper,
promoting children’s literacy is a key component of our Masonic Family Resource
Center. We are working to have books in the hands of our Masonic children
and promoting literacy in the name of Freemasonry. We are planning an online
Masonic readers club where children can post reviews, obtain recommendations
for books, participate in contests, apply for free books and engage in other
activities meant to stimulate interest in reading.
MASONIC CENTER FOR CHILDREN AND FAMILIES
As part of our long-term planning, we completed a strategic business
plan focused on developing services that better reflect the needs of our fraternal
family and achieve our vision of “supporting people’s journey through life
transition”. To that end, the Board of Trustees recently approved a plan to open a
new Masonic Center for Children and Families in Northern California.
The new Masonic Center for Children and Families (MCCF) will provide
comprehensive educational and psychological testing and treatment to children
and families in need. The MCCF center will address a shortage of effective
testing and assessment for children and adolescents and the lack of an integrated,
comprehensive and multi-disciplinary approach to care by offering the following:
Comprehensive testing and assessment. This is a unique, in-demand
service and will be the hallmark of our service and position us as a
“Center of Excellence”. Such services will offer our clients the profound
relief of being known and our clinicians the information necessary to
focus their treatment, while laying the foundation for far-reaching and
nuanced research. Individuals will receive a comprehensive assessment
at the outset of their treatment, which will guide their treatment plan and
allow us to track the efficacy of our care.

254

Proceedings of the
Interdisciplinary team approach. Currently families in need must
battle their way through a confusing and fragmented mental health
system and are often forced to rely on a single provider’s subjective
assessment of the issues and treatment. Our model calls for the test
results to be reviewed by a multi-disciplinary team that includes a
psychiatrist, psychologist, family therapist, social worker and financial
planner/community resource expert. A comprehensive treatment plan
will be developed that addresses the child’s academic, social, and
emotional needs within the context of his/her family system.
Integrated treatment management allowing us to serve member
across the State. Treatment Managers will be assigned to each child/
family and integrate the work of individual, family and group providers,
while coordinating the necessary academic and community resources.
Such a treatment management approach will differentiate our services
from all others and positions us to serve our fraternal family members
across the state. Families in need can come to our center for their
testing, assessment and treatment planning (and stay at the former
administrator’s house in Union City). The treatment manager will then
develop, coordinate and implement the proposed treatment plan in their
home community. This includes identifying qualified providers and
overseeing their treatment and the child’s progress.
Commitment to outcome studies and thoughtful research. Yale
University, Baylor University (Menninger’s) and the Anna Freud Centre
have all expressed a great interest in partnering with us to conduct
sophisticated and longitudinal research. These universities all have
well-funded research programs and are interested in using our clinical
data to address the fundamental question of “What works for whom?” In
this way, the work of our Center will become associated in the public’s
mind as offering unparalleled and comprehensive services and in the
professional arena as one of the leading clinical centers involved in
cutting-edge research and clinical design.

We are planning to open our doors in November 2010 and will be
communicating regularly with our fraternity on our progress and the benefits and
services available to them.
MASONIC OUTREACH SERVICES
As you might imagine, many of our elderly brethren and their wives
and widows have been hard hit by recent economic events and as a result, we are
serving more members through our MOS program than ever before in our history.
This year, approximately 185 members received direct financial assistance through
our MOS program; another 200 received information and referral or ongoing case

Grand Lodge F. & A.M. of California

255

management services. The generous support of Masons who give to our annual
fund help ensure that we are always in a position to the meet the needs of our
members.
A goal for the upcoming year is to streamline our intake process so that
all of our members know exactly who and how to call for help. We are planning
a significant communications campaign to ensure that all of our members know
about our services and how to access them.
All in all, it has been an extremely busy and productive year for our
Masonic Outreach and Children’s departments focused on long-term planning to
improve our services to our fraternal family and meet the needs of our members.
We are creating a service infrastructure and program base that better reflects the
needs of our membership across the generational spectrum. We look forward to
the work ahead.
Respectfully submitted,
Steffani Kizziar, Executive Director

256

Proceedings of the

Report of the Administration
and Campus Health Services
On behalf of the residents and staff of the Masonic Homes at Covina,
we are pleased to present the following Annual Report highlighting the successes
and challenges of the past year. As you read our report we hope you will sense our
continued commitment to excellence as we serve our residents and families. We
would like to also express our sincere appreciation to the Board of Trustees and
our Masonic Lodges for their dedication and faithful support of our community.
During the past year we served a senior population which averaged 78
residents and represented a resident mix of 60% Independent Living, 29% Assisted
Living and 11% Skilled Nursing. Over the same period, we had 15 admissions, 13
deaths with no transfers to our Union City Campus.
Our Waiting List included 25 prospective residents representing 22
households. The average waiting period for admission was approximately
31 months. Our staff is committed to call each prospective applicant monthly
to inquire how they are doing both physically and financially. Referrals to our
Masonic Outreach Program are made if services and support are needed.
Our Senior Community offers a “Continuing Care” model of care that
provides a full spectrum of care and services. Each resident is treated with respect
and dignity as they “age in place” in a safe and caring environment. Our campus
does not have a skilled nursing facility or memory care site. To support the skilled
nursing needs of our residents, we have established special transfer arrangements
with our Union City campus and with four high quality skilled nursing facilities in
our surrounding community for those residents who would like to remain near the
Covina campus. Although our residents reside off campus, we remain involved
in the oversight of their care. Our staff and residents visit the skilled nursing
facilities regularly. We conduct routine quality reviews to assure high quality of
care for our residents.
In further focusing on the health and safety of our residents, we continue
to monitor and measure quality indicators which include falls, unexpected weight
changes, hospital and emergency room visits, changes in Activity of Daily Living
and Incidental Activities of Daily Living. By tracking this data we are better able
to identify trends and monitor changes in residents’ health. Our findings also
enable us to improve the effectiveness of our resident needs and service planning
process.
In April of 2009, we conducted our Annual Resident Satisfaction Survey.
For the sixth consecutive year our overall resident satisfaction survey scores
were well above Holleran’s national benchmark. We had 100% of our residents
participating in the survey. The overall satisfaction scores for Independent Living

Grand Lodge F. & A.M. of California

257

were 92.4 and 91.6 for Assisted Living. Both scores were above Holleran’s
national benchmark. Our top performing strengths include friendliness and
courtesy of staff, variety and quality of suitable resident activities, confidence
in emergency medical response and approachability of administration regarding
questions and concerns.
Through feedback from our many resident committees, we have
developed and implemented action plans focused on areas in dining services and
housekeeping services. As a team of staff we have adopted a custom service culture
and continue to implement our Masonic Homes Service Excellence programs.
This specially designed program reflects the Masonic Values of Brotherly love,
Relief and Truth and teaches customer service skills to all our staff.
Our campus is proud to be the training site for two programs. The East
San Gabriel Occupational and Technical program and the Masters in Social Work
program of USC. The senior side of our campus provides in home patient care
training for Certified Nurses Assistants who are furthering their careers in home
health care. The Social Workers have the opportunity to train in the geriatric field
while spending time with our seniors.
On June 14, 2009 our campus enjoyed our 4th successful Summer
Festival. Our Summer Festival joins together our Masonic Lodges and affiliated
Masonic bodies for a time of fellowship, great food, entertainment and fun. Our
theme this year was “Flag Day.”
During the summer months, our staff and residents are busy hosting
BBQs and providing campus tours for our local Masonic Lodges, affiliated groups
and organizations. The seniors have the opportunity to interact with our guests
and show off their apartments.
On July 21, 2009 we held our Annual Volunteer Luncheon. We gave
recognition to 95 volunteers for a total of 12,911 hours of service this past year.
Many of our senior residents, R.O.P. students, Interns and outside community
residents actively volunteer.
This year we have completed several significant capital projects to
improve our living environment and the quality of life for our residents. The
project list includes; replacement of the cooling tower, heat pumps, upgrading the
nurse call system to wireless, installation of an energy efficient heater for the pool
and remodeling of the public restrooms.

258

Proceedings of the

As a resident centered, mission driven community, we are dedicated and
committed to carry out our mission to provide quality care to California Master
Masons, their wives, widows and mothers with dignity and compassion within an
atmosphere based on Masonic tenants, which fulfill the physical, social, spiritual
and nurturing needs of our extended community.
Respectfully submitted
Judy Figueroa, Administrator

Proceedings of the

Report of the Trustees of the
California Masonic Foundation
To the Most Worshipful Grand Lodge, F.&A.M. of California:
This Annual Communication marks the Fortieth Anniversary of the
California Masonic Foundation, and I am pleased to report on the continued
growth and accomplishments of the Foundation.
This year we held the first ever strategic planning retreat of the
Foundation, to reflect on the past, and sharpen our focus on future plans. Your
Board of Trustees are not merely stewards of Foundation dollars, but stewards
in the important work in assisting the Brethren of this jurisdiction in making
California, and the world, a better place.
In 1969, the Foundation held assets of $337,000, and awarded
scholarships totaling $16,000. Today, we hold over $23,000,000 in assets, and
awarded this year nearly $775,000 in scholarships. Today, our annual program
budget exceeds $1,000,000, and supports programs not even existing in 1969.
We continue our long-standing connection with public education, providing
training for public school teachers through the Masonic Model Student Assistance
Program. We promote Lodge growth through the Masonic Formation education
series. We fund the Institute for Masonic Studies and underwrite the annual
Masonic Symposium. We work to improve our constituent Lodge leadership
by underwriting the Wardens Leadership Retreats, and the Lodge Management
Certification Program. By underwriting the Child I.D. Program, we assist Lodges
in providing this important service to their local community.
While this jurisdiction has a historical role in supporting public education,
the Foundation this year assisted in beginning the restoration of our prominence.
On April 1st, our Grand Master started Public Schools Month in the Visitacion
Valley area of San Francisco, awarding the school district funds totaling $30,000
provided by San Francisco Lodges and the Foundation. Moreover, the Mayors
Education Roundtable in the San Francisco area will not meet without a Masonic
representative at the table. Brethren, it is incumbent on each of us, in our own
community, to restore our role established early in the last century, that no school
holds a meeting regarding education issues without a Mason at the table.
This year also shows our growth in extending the gentle hand of
Freemasonry into the community. Our state and nation suffers from a shortage
in nursing personnel with advanced degrees and training. Our response to that
need is the Claire V. Cunningham Masonic Fund for Leadership in Nursing. We
are now partnered with our California Masonic Homes, the California Shrine

Grand Lodge F. & A.M. of California

263

Hospitals, and the Association of California Nurse Leaders to provide annual
scholarships for advanced degrees in nursing education and administration.
Moreover, we have partnered with three California State University campuses to
provide scholarships for the nurse cohort training program, again for advanced
degrees in nursing. Over the next several years, we will be making a positive
impact on the quality of nursing care in this state.
Our gentle hand this year also reached out to underprivileged children
in the San Francisco area. The Masons-4-Mitts initiative, in collaboration with
the Junior Giants Foundation, raised over $27,000 from local Lodges to provide
baseball mitts. Our Grand Master was honored to present this money on behalf
of the Brethren during home plate ceremonies at a recent Giants baseball game.
We plan to extend this program to other areas in the state with similar foundations
and help more children turn from gangs to organized sports to occupy their time.
Finally, our Grand Master this year undertook the creation of Masonic
Studies curriculum at UCLA, under the guidance of Professor Margaret Jacob.
The Foundation is proud to assist in this one-of-a-kind undertaking in serving
as the fundraising source to see that this worthwhile project is endowed with
the necessary funding. Over the coming years, respected academics with proper
credentials will frame to the public exactly what Freemasonry is, and is not, rather
than non-masons telling fictional stories about mythical gold treasures hidden by
Knights Templar.
It is important to note in this report that the linchpin of the Foundation
Board, and the biggest cheerleader in providing the Masonic Model Student
Assistance Program, has retired. Mrs. Ellen Coll provided countless hours of
support to the Board, and her gentle presence will be deeply missed by school
teachers and administrators up and down this state. In fact, all Brethren in this
jurisdiction have benefited from her work, always coming from the heart. We
wish Ellen all the best in spending more time with her grandchildren.
Additionally, Worshipful Brother Myron Tisdel has tenured out this year.
Brother Myron has been an important guiding hand for the past nine years, and
his counsel will be missed. I especially thank him for his hard work leading our
scholarship efforts, with long hours spent gleaning the best of the best that have
applied to the Foundation scholarship program.
My Brethren, your Foundation is firmly committed to extend on your
behalf the gentle hand of Freemasonry for years to come. No longer must we
look at history with fondness, for right now, this time, our time, is a great time to
be a Mason.

Proceedings of the

Respectfully submitted,
John F. Lowe, President
Myron A.Tisdel, Vice-President

Report of the Board of Trustees
of the California Masonic Memorial Temple
Most Worshipful Grand Master and members of the Grand Lodge:
This afternoon I am pleased to make the 55th annual report of the
California Masonic Memorial Temple since its incorporation in 1954.
The CMMT owns this great edifice in which we meet today and derives
tax-exempt revenue by leasing its space to the Grand Lodge of California whose
offices are on the third floor; to the Nob Hill Masonic Center that operates the
garage; the telecommunications facility on the roof and to other third parties
who lease space in the building. We also receive tax-exempt contributions from
candidates who are initiated into our California lodges. We are very happy that
this number continues to grow each year. With the revenue streams in such flux
as they were this year, this Board was most prudent in the expenses for operating
and maintaining this building and we look to an increase in building use in order
to fund necessary improvements in the future.
Upgrade of the Auditorium, California Room and Exhibit Hall
A good deal of our efforts this year has been devoted to implementing
our vision to keep your facility viable for the future and competitive in the ever
changing market in which we operate. Working with several business partners,
we have embarked this year on a plan to optimize the utilization of this building,
enable us to meet our current and future operating needs, and provide the means
for the increasing costs of maintenance, repair, and restoration. The result will be
to create this auditorium into the premiere performance venue in San Francisco,
make the California Room a viable and welcome respite on Nob Hill and transform
the Exhibit Hall and kitchen into a world class restaurant, all while maintaining
our beautiful War Memorial for all time; still serve the needs for our Annual
Communication and Grand Lodge while maintaining a positive relationship with
our neighbors.
Upgrade of the Parking Garage
We have redesigned the entrance to the parking garage to improve traffic
flow on California Street and accommodate the increased usage of this building.
At the same time we are developing ways to decrease noise pollution from the
various vendors who rent ouf facility.
On behalf of the Board of Trustees of the California Masonic Memorial
Temple, I wish to extend our gratitude to the Grand Secretary and the staff who

266

Proceedings of the

work each day to maintain this building and manage its business so effectively.
We wish also to thank the Grand Master, Most Worshipful Larry L. Adamson, for
the opportunity to serve in this capacity.
Respectfully submitted,
Myron A. Tisdel, President
David D. Johnson, Vice President

Report of the Board of Directors
of the Nob Hill Masonic Center, Inc.
To the Most Worshipful Grand Lodge, F. & A. M. of California:
Nob Hill Masonic Center, Inc. is the for profit, income tax paying entity
which oversees the parking garage at the Nob Hill Masonic Center and until
January 1, 2009, also managed the operations of the Auditorium and Exhibit
Hall. On January 1, 2009 Nob Hill relinquished management of the Auditorium
and Exhibit hall back to C.M.M.T., who at the same time entered into a lease
agreement with Live Nation to operate the Auditorium and Exhibit Hall.
Due to the transfer of responsibility for the Auditorium and Exhibit Hall
it became unnecessary for Nob Hill to retain the nine full time employees as
well as part time box office staff. One employee, the Building Engineer, was
transferred to the Grand Lodge payroll effective January 1, 2009. All other
employees of Nob Hill were laid off after adopting and implementing a severance
policy similar to that used by the Masonic Homes of California. This was not an
easy thing to do as some of staff had been working in this building for several
years and we appreciate the many hours they all spent maintaining and operating
this beautiful building.
Use of the Auditorium was less than normal during November and
December in anticipation of the turnover to Live Nation, which was originally
anticipated to occur earlier in 2009. This reduction in use of the Auditorium has
continued throughout 2009, thereby reducing garage revenues. In addition garage
revenues for the year ending October 31, 2009 are lower than anticipated due to
a drop in monthly parking primarily resulting from the economic downturn and
increased apartment vacancies.
Income and expenses through June 30, 2009, plus projected income and
expenses through October 31, 2009, based on unaudited reports are as follows:
Total Revenue............................................................$1,487,826
Less:
Total Cost of Services................................................$1,055,643
General & Administrative Expenses............................$416,447
Total Expenses...........................................................$1,472,090
Earnings From Operations..............................................$15,736
Retained Earnings from previous years will enable Nob Hill to cover
expenses and pay a dividend to Grand Lodge in Fiscal 2010.

Proceedings of the
Report of the Committee
On Finance

To the Most Worshipful Grand Lodge, F & A.M. of California:
The California Masonic Code requires the Committee on Finance to
review all of the proposed Recommendations and Resolutions to be considered at
the Annual Communication and to report on the financial impact these items may
have on the Grand Lodge budget.
Earlier this year, the committee reviewed the Carry-Over and New
Resolutions as provided by the Grand Secretary. On July 14, we finalized our
report on the cost impact of each item and it was mailed to the lodges by the Grand
Secretary on July 27 with the legislation. The committee subsequently reviewed
Grand Master Adamson’s Decisions and Recommendations and found that none
of those items will have a financial impact on the Grand Lodge budget.
Because only three of the new Resolutions have any financial impact at
all, and the impact is very minimal, and in as much as our written report has been
distributed, I will report on them now and not later during the session.
Resolution No. 09-01: Creates the Hiram’s Pillar Award: The certificates
will cost the Grand Lodge approximately $500. The resolution stipulates that the
medallion and pin will be made available by Grand Lodge for purchase, but the
potential cost to Grand Lodge is the expense of any unsold, returned, or damaged
medallions and pins.
Resolution No. 09-03: Expands Maximum Size and Composition
of Grand Lodge Committee On Investments: The costs of the Grand Lodge
Committee on Investments is shared between all of the entities with invested
funds. The expansion of this committee will cost the Grand Lodge approximately
$300.
Resolution No. 09-08: Entitles Secretary and Inspector to a Vote at Grand
Lodge: The increased number of eligible voters at the Annual Communication
will require that more ballots be printed and counted. These additional ballots
will cost the Grand Lodge approximately $200.
Again, in the view of the Committee on Finance, none of the other
legislation will have an impact on the Grand Lodge budget.

David L. Barnekoff
John R. Gale

Respectfully submitted,
Thomas E. Kurth, Chairman
Timothy A. Wood, Vice Chairman

Levon H. Keshishian
Lester W. Miller

Report of the Committee
on Fraternal Support Services
To the Most Worshipful Grand Lodge, F.& A.M. of California:
The FSSC operates under Section 406.040 of the CMC which calls for
a committee of not less than five nor more than 15 members. The intent is to
have at least one member from each of the Grand Lodge Divisions. Currently the
committee has seven members, with representation from all but Divisions 2 and 6.
According to the CMC, the committee shall:
Provide information about fraternal support services available to lodges
and their members and widows. These fraternal support services include those
available through the Masonic Homes of California, the California Masonic
Foundation, the Grand Lodge, Masonic Service Bureaus and any subsidiaries
thereof; the committee has contacted all of the above entities and received contact
information and appropriate text to be included in the Ambassador Guide and used
in email communications with the Lodge Ambassadors. The Ambassador Guide
was revised in 2009 and electronically distributed to the Lodge Ambassadors,
placed on the committee website www.fraternalsupport.org and linked to the
publications section of www.freemason.org. The committee is dependent upon
receiving current information from the various entities for distribution. It does not
produce the information.
Provide programs and other resources for Lodges to identify, maintain
contact with, and provide assistance to Masonic widows and members in need; the
committee has requested, and will continually repeat the request, for Ambassadors
to send “best practices” case studies of how their outreach efforts are making a
difference. As these are received they are shared with other Ambassadors through
“The Leader” and are placed in a resource section of the www.fraternalsupport.
org website. The committee is developing a quarterly report format from the
Lodge Ambassadors to the FSSC to provide statistical and anecdotal information
about these efforts.
Develop program materials for use by the Lodges in their activities in
support of public schools, the observance of the United States Constitution and
such other Lodge programs as may from time to time be approved by the Grand
Master; this is probably the weakest area of the FSSC at the present time. The
committee is reviewing the currently available materials and has, again, requested
that the Lodge Ambassadors provide input on successful programs at the Lodge
level for the committee to include on the website and in “The Leader.” There have
been few responses to that request, but will continue to suggest it. The FSSC would
welcome input from any other committee, for example, we have been contacted to
provide assistance in the “Pass it On” program. As a result, an “elevator speech”

274

Proceedings of the

was sent to the Lodge Ambassadors to use in their stated meetings as a means of
encouraging more participation in the program.
Encourage the appointment of an Ambassador in each Lodge to serve as
a liaison between the committee and the membership of his Lodge. This function
is an ongoing process for the committee. The roster is constantly changing,
particularly as new Masters are installed. Currently there are Ambassadors in 334
of the 335 Lodges on our roster.
The committee has made presentations at all four Warden’s Retreats to
familiarize the Wardens with the purpose and activity of the Ambassador program.
The committee, in addition to revising the Ambassador Guide, has
developed a self-administered test of the material in the Guide (and the specific
role and functions of the Ambassador position) and will be rolled out to the
Ambassadors in the next Masonic year. This is a self-correcting exam which
will serve as a tutorial as well as a test. Upon successful completion of the test,
the committee will issue a Certificate of Completion, suitable for framing, to be
signed by the Lodge Master and presented to the Ambassador at a stated meeting.
All Ambassadors receive a badge identifying them as a Lodge Ambassador and
are encouraged to wear them at stated meetings. In the coming year the committee
will be developing a simple business card for the Ambassadors containing vital
contact information. They can use this to disseminate information about the social
safety net programs of the fraternity to members in need of these services.
The Fraternal Support Services Committee supports the Grand Lodge
Strategic Plan.
Respectfully submitted:
Michael D. Neben, Chairman

Report of the Committee
on Institute for Masonic Studies
To the Most Worshipful Grand Lodge, F. & A.M. of California
This report provides information as to the activities of the Institute
for Masonic Studies for the year 2008–2009. The Institute is an agency of the
Grand Lodge of California that seeks to educate the Freemasons and the general
community about the philosophy, values and tenets of Freemasonry and enrich our
understanding of its symbolism and rituals. Regular meetings are held quarterly
during the fiscal year.

Grand Lodge F. & A.M. of California

275

The Institute is charged with providing resources to encourage the study
of Freemasonry and oversees programs of the Grand Lodge created to accomplish
these activities.
These responsibilities include the development and implementation of
Masonic Formation programs including Masonic symposiums, membership and
candidate education materials and lodge Masonic education training programs;
the promotion and development of Masonic Formation Committees within
individual lodges in this jurisdiction; and stewardship over the Henry Wilson Coil
Library and Museum.
During this year the following actions were accomplished:
•

As part of the Conference of Grand Masters of North America annual
meeting in February 2009 at Anaheim a special exhibit (Builders of
Dreams) was presented which included artifacts with banners and a
video presentation of California Masonry.

•

With support from the Heritage Fund by the California Masonic
Foundation, renovation and expansion of the storage capabilities of the
archive room at Grand Lodge supporting the effort of the Henry Wilson
Coil Library and Museum to preserve, restore and exhibit the historic
books and records in its possession was accomplished.

•

The curator, Adam G. Kendall, of the Library and Museum attended
and presented a paper at the International Conference on the History of
Freemasonry (ICHF) in Edinburgh, Scotland. This was the first time
any brother from California has made a presentation and Adam’s was
well received.

•

The 2009 California Symposium, Applying Masonry in Contemporary
Society, was held on May 2, 2009 at Dodd Hall on the campus of
he University of California at Los Angeles. Reservations for the
event exceeded 300 people. Presenters included Dr. Margaret Jacob,
Most Worshipful R. Stephen Doan and Dr. John L. Cooper III. The
symposium concluded with a panel discussion on how Masonry can
provide leadership in bridging contemporary society’s divisions.

•

Masonic Formation workshops were conducted in San Diego, Fresno,
Sacramento, and San Francisco. Based on member suggestions and
surveys taken the Masonic Formation Mentorship Manuel is undergoing
a revision process for release during 2010.

•

Completion of the Fellow Craft DVD is expected for release at this
communication with the Master Mason DVD planned for 2010.

•

The Institute provided administration and oversight support to the
Grand Master for his UCLA project to provide two fellowships. One

276

Proceedings of the
post doctoral for developing and teaching a curriculum on the origins of
Freemasonry focused on Europe. The second Graduate–level fellowship
will provide to a candidate who is researching the origins of Freemasonry
in the United States.

The Institute acknowledges with gratitude the support it has received
from the Grand Master, Grand Secretary and Grand Lodge office staff during this
year.
Respectfully Submitted,
A. Glenn MacBride, Chairman
R. Stephen Doan PGM Vice–Chairman
Brian P. Bezner

Report of the Committee
on Insurance
To the Most Worshipful Grand Lodge, F.& A.M. of California:
The Insurance Committee reports directly to the Grand Secretary on all
insurance matters with the exception of the Life and Medical Coverage and other
benefits for the employees.
There are essentially three meetings each Masonic year which follow
a more or less standard pattern. Other meetings may be called by the Grand
Secretary as needed and on occasion it could be only one or two members of the
committee present.
The three meetings are as follows;
1.

This meeting takes place in November each year and both parties, the
Grand Lodge Insurance Committee representatives along with the Grand
Secretary or the Grand Lodge CFO and the Broker. The Grand Lodge
Representative outlines the plans for the upcoming year along with any
changes that may be taking place. The broker on the other hand details
the insurance marketing conditions and consults with the Insurance
Committee representative on the strategy to take place and set the date
for the official quotes to be presented for review.

2.

The next meeting is with the full Insurance committee to review the
quotes from the broker and this takes place usually in February. Once
the Grand Secretary has the approval of the Insurance Committee, he
makes the final decision on the quotes presented taking into account the
recommendations of the Committee. The policies are then ordered.

Grand Lodge F. & A.M. of California
3.

277

A June meeting is usually established which is attended by the Insurance
Committee who receive all the policies an review each and every one
to make certain not only pricing is what was quoted but that all entities
are included and the broadest possible coverage provided with the least
amount of restrictions.

We are pleased to report that the pricing has been kept to a minimum for
not only the Grand Lodge but also the Hall Associations. Recommendations were
also made to provide the Hall Associations and the Lodges with standard Lease
Agreements and to assure all parties were protected with proper names, terms and
limits. Too, a short term Lease Policy is being worked on to assist the Lodges
and Hall Associations with inexpensive policies available to those who rent the
facilities for such this as weddings or outside business meetings to name a couple.
The Insurance Committee thus far is happy with the service being
provided by the Broker and continues to monitor that performance.
Respectfully submitted,
Stuart A. Wright, Chairman
Richard Wright, Vice Chairman
Leo W. Anselm

Berry L. Griffin, Jr.

Report of the Committee
on Investments
To the Most Worshipful Grand Lodge, F. & A. M. of California:
Overview
The Investment Committee is charged with the prudent handling of
Grand Lodge investment funds. These funds consist primarily of the General
and Endowment funds for the Masonic Homes of California, but also include the
investment portfolios of the California Masonic Foundation, California Masonic
Memorial Temple, the Grand Lodge Retirement plan and the Grand Lodge
itself. We take this charge very seriously. In its simplest terms, we manage the
investment funds with an eye both for growth and preservation of capital. We
strive to maintain a diversified portfolio that is designed to generate the necessary
funds for the continuing operation of the Masonic functions as well as allow for
growth for future needs. In doing so, we constantly scan the horizon in an attempt
to manage the funds to take advantage of long-term trends and opportunities.
The Grand Lodge Investment Committee utilizes the services of professional
investment management firms to gain access to the capital markets. Funds are
primarily invested in U.S. and international equity and fixed income markets,

278

Proceedings of the

although we have recently embarked on investing in less traditional vehicles
which are not as correlated to the stock and bond markets.
The capital markets in which the Grand Lodge invests had negative
performance over the past fiscal year, however, the 4th quarter showed significant
improvement which continued into the 1st and 2nd quarters of this year.
Grand Lodge Investment Performance
For the year ended 6-30-09
Our U.S. equity portfolio was down 28.3% compared to -26.6% for the
Russell 3000 Index. The Non-U.S. equity portfolio was down 30.9% compared to
-31.4% for the MSCI EAFE Index. Global Equities were down 23.7% compared
to -29.3% for the MSCI All-Country World Index. The fixed income portfolio was
up 6.3% compared to a gain in the Barclays bond Index of 6.0%. Our Alternative
Investments were down 15.8% compared to -15.1% for the HFR Fund of Funds
Index. Finally the Global Tactical Assets Investments were down 7.0% compared
to its benchmark of a positive 3.5%. Performance in the second half of 2008 was
very negative. While returns in the first half of 2009 were mixed, performance
began to rebound in the 4th quarter of 2008. Detailed manager performance can
be found in the tables following this report.
The U.S. and world economies have just endured the longest and most
severe downturn since the Great Depression. The recession was initiated by an
extended period of excessive credit across the globe and a collapse in U.S. housing
prices. While it is too soon to pronounce the worst is behind us, it is probably fair
to say that a world-wide meltdown in the financial system was averted. Among
other questions, investors are now trying to determine what long-term impact
these efforts to save the economy will be? The Federal Reserve has kept interest
rates near zero in an effort to stimulate the economy, while keeping an eye to
preventing a resurgence of inflation. This has created an extremely confusing
fixed income marketplace.
These mixed long-term results are attributable to both the U.S. Equity
and International Equity managers. The relative over-weighting to U.S. Equity
value managers has hurt us in the three-year time frame, but in the longer term, it
has benefited the fund. Our international investment manager has had difficulty
over the last few years in stock and sector selection, but the last few quarters have
been favorable and their investment selection seems to back on track. The bond
manager continues to generate alpha for the portfolio.
The Investment Committee does not attempt to take advantage of
perceived short-term anomalies in the markets by chasing the most recent top
performing asset class. Rather, it arrives at its recommended allocation to stocks
and bonds and other asset classes only after very careful due diligence and
consideration. The purpose of the funds, their spending requirements, investment

Grand Lodge F. & A.M. of California

279

time horizon and ability to assume risk are all factored into the recommendations.
The possibility and duration of potential losses are carefully weighed against the
long-term potential for appreciation of assets after withdrawals are taken into
account. The Investment Committee recommends asset allocations that will
provide the highest probability that the funds will be able to meet their respective
objectives over the long-term.
Grand Lodge & Related Entity Investment Performance
For the year ended 6-30-09
The market value of the Masonic Home’s portfolio as of June 30, 2009,
was $600.9 million compared to $783.7 million a year earlier. This, combined with
the investment portfolios of the other entities which the Investment Committee
advises, resulted in combined assets totaling slightly more than $642.5 million
compared to $837.5 a year ago.
As of June 30, 2009, the combined funds of Grand Lodge and the related
Masonic entities were down 19.8% for the twelve-month period. This compares
to -21.0% for the custom benchmark index.
For the three-year period ending June 30th, the combined Grand Lodge
and related entities funds were down 4.2% versus -4.1% for the benchmark.
Since inception, however, our funds have returned 7.0% annually versus the 6.8%
return of the benchmark. Detailed Entity performance can be found in the tables
following this report.
Grand Lodge Investment Management
For the year ended 6-30-09
In order to fulfill our responsibilities in accordance with the Grand Lodge
Investment Policy and Philosophy, the Investment Committee meets quarterly
with our Investment Consultant, Ennis/Knupp and our individual portfolio
managers. We review their respective performance compared to various indices
representing their objective. This process provides the Investment Committee
with the necessary data to adequately evaluate each investment manager on a
timely basis, and to observe whether they continue to achieve the objectives of
their mandate.
In Fiscal 2009, the Committee invested in TALF (Term Asset-Backed
Securities Loan Facility) sponsored by the U.S. Government. In Addition the
Committee added PIMCO All Asset All Authority to the portfolio. Also during the
year, the Committee terminated its relationship with Legg Mason (a U.S. equity
manager). Finally, on April 1, 2009 the Committee transferred our custodian
relationship to Mellon Bank from the Bank of New York.
Not only is the Investment Committee responsible for managing the
portfolios of our various organizations, but it also oversees the management of our

280

Proceedings of the

planned gift investments. The current portfolio of planned gifts includes nearly
$10 million, including $7.5 million in charitable trusts and just over $2 million
in charitable gift annuities. Despite the obvious difficulties in our economy, the
Grand Lodge fundraising staff had another successful year in the solicitation
of planned gifts. Particular recognition should go to Richard “Dick” Kellogg,
our Planned Giving Officer, who raised more than $600,000 in charitable gift
annuities from brethren interested in leaving a charitable legacy this year. The
Investment Committee is grateful to the entire staff at the offices of Philanthropy
and Finance for their support and hard work this past year, and look forward to
another successful philanthropic campaign in 2009-2010.
Your Grand Lodge Investment Committee is also available to assist
individual lodges in determining appropriate investment strategy. One available
vehicle to aid in this effort is the “Investment Options for California Masonic
Lodges” program which is intended to assist Lodges in determining an appropriate
investment strategy and asset allocation. We are also here to help Lodges establish
a set of policies and procedures that are designed to ensure assets are invested
according to the needs of the Lodge and to safeguard against the misuse of funds.
We direct your attention to the “Investment Guidelines” document in particular,
which is available on the web site. Investment Committee members continue to
make themselves available to the Lodges for assistance with this program and
other questions regarding lodge investments.
Summary
The years during which I have had the honor to serve on the Committee
and now as Chairman have been very rewarding. I have learned much and am
very grateful to have had the opportunity to lead such a fine group of men on the
Grand Lodge Investment Committee. I thank you for the opportunity and wish to
express my appreciation to the Committee for the commitment and devotion they
have demonstrated for the Masonic causes. They have always been forthright
with their opinions and expertise. I am convinced we have the finest group of
dedicated investment professionals fulfilling our very important mission.

Proceedings of the
Report of the Committee
on Jurisprudence

To the Most Worshipful Grand Lodge, F. & A.M. of California:
The Committee on Jurisprudence serves as the legal adviser to the
Grand Master and the legal adviser to the Grand Lodge when in session. The
Committee reviews proposed resolutions and recommendations to be considered
at the next Annual Communication and prepares any necessary legislation to carry
out the intent of the proponents. At the Annual Communication, it comments
on the legality of the Grand Master’s decisions and dispensations and on the
legal effect of the proposed recommendations and resolutions. After the Annual
Communication, it prepares the changes to the California Masonic Code to reflect
the legislation which was adopted.
In the Committee’s view, those of Grand Master Adamson’s decisions
and dispensations coming to its attention were legal and within his discretion.
In the Committee’s view, the legislation mailed by the Grand Secretary on July
27th of this year is sufficient to carry out the intent of the proponents, and the
legislation contained in Grand Master Adamson’s recommendations which he
will present in his message is sufficient to carry out his intent. We believe that
each piece of this legislation has the legal effect set forth in it.
The legislation mailed by the Grand Secretary on July 27th of this year
and the legislation contained in Grand Master Adamson’s recommendations
require no separate motion because they are automatically before this Annual
Communication. The presiding officer will announce the vote required for the
passage of each item before discussion begins. Any legislation which does not
amend the California Masonic Code or the Ritual requires a simple majority only
for adoption. Any amendment to the California Masonic Code or the Ritual must
be adopted by a five-sixths affirmative vote. If the amendment receives a simple
majority but less than a five-sixths affirmative vote, it carries over to the next
Annual Communication when only a two-thirds affirmative vote is required for
adoption.
We thank the Grand Master for the opportunity which he has given us to
serve this year.

Mark K. Ameli
David M. Ferreria
Gerhard W. Luck

Respectfully submitted,
Allen B. Gresham, PGM, Chairman

R. Stephen Doan, PGM, Consultant

Jeffrey L. Bear
Jack R. Levitt, P.G.M.
Paul D. Rosenberger

Report of the Committee
on Membership Development
To the Most Worshipful Grand Lodge, F. & A.M. of California:
My name is Gary L. Peterson, and I am a Past Master of Saddleback
Laguna Lodge No 672, and Chairman of the Membership Development
Committee.
It is my pleasure to present to you the Annual Report of the Membership
Development Committee for the 2008-2009 Masonic year.
I would first like to acknowledge and thank the committeemen and Grand
Lodge staff members who throughout this year have continued to contribute their
time and energy in supporting the development of this Committees successful
programs. Those committee members are Wor. Gary L. Peterson, Past Master of
Saddleback Laguna Lodge No 672; Wor Anil Comelo , Past Master of Navel Lodge
No 87: Wor Rick Keltner, PM Union Kit Carson Lodge No 58 ; Wor Frederick
Votto, Past Master of Liberal Arts Lodge No 677; Wor. Sean Finney, Past Master
of Orinda Lodge No 122; Wor. Bruce McKelvie, Past Master of Mission Lodge
No 169; Bro Gerald Petievich, Senior Warden of San Marino Lodge No 408; Wor.

286

Proceedings of the

Jeffrey Schimsky, PM, Master of Los Cerritos Lodge No 674; Wor. John Cover
Spear, Past Master of Moreno Valley Lodge No 804.
The Grand Lodge staff members are: Wor. James E. Banta, Manager
Member Services and a Past Master of Fresno Lodge No 247, and Terry Mendez,
Director of Communications, and we particularly thank them for their work on
behalf of our Fraternity.
Additionally, Brother Erik Borg, a member of Saddleback-Laguna No
682 has been very helpful in the management and operation of our booth during
Grand Lodge.
Again this year the “Pass It On” program continued its new member
pattern of increasing numbers through the comprehensive program. Presentations
of the program were made at the Lodge Management Certification events,
as well as through the use of MDC booths at Grand Lodge Annual Communication
and Wardens Retreats. A major project completed this year was the revision and
updating of the PIO program.
I am pleased to report to you that we again set a record for new membership
within our Jurisdiction. The year end shows 1246 new men raised as Master Masons
with many lodges raising over our goal of 5 per lodge. Through the use of Grand
Lodge’s membership programs, we believe it reflects a growth in new Masons that
should continue.
The Committee again, presented a Certificate of Excellence to each lodge
that attained the “Pass It On” goal of five or more new Master Masons raised during
the year. In addition a traveling plaque was presented to the lodge which raised the
most Master Masons.
The MDC, in specific requested instances, partnered with the Lodge
Support Committee with efforts to assist troubled lodges survive. We will continue
to assist the LSC when and where needed.
At the suggestion of the Executive Committee, the MDC analyzed the
SPND issue as to its causes, and its effects on membership as a whole. With this
in mind, a program from Grand Lodge staff will be instituted to contact those
members already suspended, and attempt to bring them back into the Masonic
family. Additionally, the MDC wrote a proposed legislative amendment to the CMC
requiring the Member Retention Committee of each lodge to personally contact
each member eligible for SPND prior to actual suspension. This legislation is being
proposed at the 2009 Annual Communication.
Deputy Grand Master Nagel’s “Coming Home” project became a major
marketing and promotion project. The overall program tactics will begin to roll out
immediately after the conclusion of the Annual Communication.
The Executive Committee requested the MDC to look into the “Failure

Grand Lodge F. & A.M. of California

287

to Advance” problem our lodges have been having, and at this writing the MDC
is still in the analysis stage of this problem.
The MDC will continue to evaluate its programs as to overall effectiveness
of the individual components. Necessary adjustments will continue to be made as
the program implementation continues.
On behalf of the Membership Development Committee for the Grand
Lodge of the State of California, I wish to thank you and the Most Worship Larry
Adamson for giving us the opportunity to update you on the progress and activities
of our committee.
Thank you.

Report of the Committee on
Trial Review

To the Most Worshipful Grand Lodge, F.& A.M. of California:
During the past Masonic year, the Grand Master invoked the automatic
expulsion provisions of California Masonic Code Section 1404.820 in ten
matters. Each of the convictions in those matters involved moral turpitude within
the meaning of CMC Section 100.075.
In each of the following matters the committee finds there was
compliance with the procedural requirements of the California Masonic Code,
that the findings were supported by the evidence and that the penalties fixed were
appropriate.
A.

In Trial No. 2551, the Accused pled no contest to two charges of
unmasonic conduct for having struck one Brother and having made
disparaging remarks about another Brother. After his investigation,
the Trial Master determined the incidents were part of a series of
escalating verbal confrontations among the parties and fixed the
penalty at Suspension.

B.

In Trial No. 2552, the Accused pled guilty to a charge of unmasonic
conduct for having made a disparaging remark about two members

288

Proceedings of the
of our Youth Orders. The Accused then wrote a letter of apology to
the youths involved and to their parents. The Trial Master fixed the
penalty at Reprimand in open Lodge.
C.

In Trial No. 2553, the Accused was found guilty by a Trial
Commission for participating in the hazing of a candidate for the
Third Degree, by among other things, punching and hitting the
candidate. The penalty was fixed at Expulsion.

D.

In Trial No. 2556, the Accused failed to enter a plea to charges that
he made blatantly racist statements to and about the Senior Warden
of his Lodge. The Trial Master found the charges proven and fixed
the penalty at Expulsion.

E.

In Trial No. 2563, the Accused was charged with failing, while he
was Master of the Lodge, to carefully monitor unlawful investment
activities made by the Accused in Trial No. 2564. The penalty was
fixed at Reprimand in open Lodge.

F.

In Trial No. 2564, the Accused, who had no formal financial training
or background, caused Lodge funds to be invested in contravention
of the requirements of the California Masonic Code. The Lodge lost
in excess of $250,000. The penalty was fixed at Suspension.

G.

In Trial No. 2569, the Accused was found guilty after a trial before
a Trial Commission of keeping for his own use fees for a Petition
for Degrees. The money was repaid. The penalty was fixed at
Suspension.

H.

In Trial No. 2573, the Accused, who attempted to “resign from
Freemasonry in California,” did not enter a plea to charges that
he failed to obey an order from the Grand Master renouncing his
membership in the group commonly referred to as the “Snakes.”
The Trial Master fixed the penalty at Expulsion.

The Committee on Trial Review therefore submits and moves for
adoption of the following Resolution:
“Resolved that in Trial Numbers 2551, 2552, 2553, 2556,
2563, 2564, 2569 and 2573 the findings and penalties fixed are
affirmed.”
I.

In Trial No. 2555, the Accused entered a plea of not guilty and
requested that his letter of explanation be considered as his defense
to the charges. The trial summons sent to the Accused did not

Grand Lodge F. & A.M. of California

289

contain the notices required under CMC Section 1404.540. In fact,
the notices that were given were misleading. Thereafter, the Trial
Master failed to conduct a trial as required by our Code. Rather he
relied solely on two hearsay letters which accompanied the charges
as the basis for his finding of guilty, setting the penalty at Expulsion.
The Committee on Trial Review therefore submits and moves for
adoption of the following Resolution:
“Resolved that in Trial Number 2555, the record be vacated,
the penalty of Expulsion reversed and the matter remanded to a
new Trial Master for further proceedings.”
The committee has reviewed an application by William Lyle Martin for
Restoration after Expulsion. He was expelled in 1987 after pleading guilty to
knowingly receiving stolen property, a crime of moral turpitude. The application
for Restoration did not follow the procedural requirements of Section 1404.1050
of the California Masonic Code.
The Committee on Trial Review therefore submits and moves for
adoption of the following Resolution:
“Resolved that the application of William Lyle Martin for
Restoration is denied and the application remanded to his
former Lodge for further proceedings in accordance with the
California Masonic Code.”

E. Dale Armstrong
Morel R. Callueng
David H. Lieberthal

Respectfully submitted,
Ralph B. Dash, Chairman

George C. Turegano

Robert E. Scott
Robert L. Sills
Louis A. Trovato

California......................................San Francisco...................................................1850
Consolidated December 2, 1975 with Occidental No. 22 as Occidental-California No.
1. Consolidated July 11, 1978 with Mt. Davidson No. 481 as Occidental-California
No. 1. Consolidated July 3, 1979 with Ingleside-Pyramid No. 562 as OccidentalCalifornia No. 1. Name changed October 23, 1997 to California No. 1.

Consolidated March 1, 1999 with Northern Light No. 190 as Western Star No. 2.

Name changed to Jennings No. 4. Charter surrendered May 6, 1853.

1859.

Consolidated December 1876 with Mount Jefferson No. 107 as Tuolumne No.
8. Consolidated December 12, 1891 with Columbia No. 28 as Tuolumne No. 8.
Consolidated September 15, 1919 with George Washington No. 62 as Tuolumne No.
8. Consolidated July 1, 1998 with East Belt No. 391 as East Belt-Tuolumne No. 8.

9

Consolidated December 1874 with Corinthian No. 69 as Corinthian No. 9.
Consolidated June 16, 1916 with Yuba No. 39 as Corinthian No. 9. Consolidated
January 1, 1997 with Hammonton No. 433 as Corinthian-Hammonton No. 9.

RECOGNITION OF FOREIGN GRAND LODGES
The Grand Lodge of California recognizes all of the Grand Lodges in the United States
published in the 2009 List of Lodges Masonic, which includes all 50 states and the District
of Columbia. In addition, the Grand Lodge of California recognizes a number of Prince
Hall Grand Lodges and foreign Grand Lodges around the world. The Grand Lodge of
California recognizes some, but not all, of the foreign Grand Lodges in the 2009 List of
Lodges Masonic as well as a number of foreign Grand Lodges that are not listed therein.
For clarity, the foreign Grand Lodges that are recognized by the Grand Lodge of California
are listed below alphabetically by country. For those that are published in the 2009 List of
Lodges Masonic, the corresponding page number is provided in parenthesis. Recognized
Grand Lodges that are not published in the 2009 List of Lodges Masonic are marked with
an asterisk. The Grand Secretary of the Grand Lodge of California is responsible for all
correspondence with these Grand Lodges and the Lodges under their jurisdiction. Please
contact his office for assistance.
ANDORRA: Symbolic Grand Lodge of Andorra*; ARGENTINA: Grand Lodge of
Argentina (116) ; ARMENIA: Grand Lodge of Armenia (117); AUSTRALIA: United
Grand Lodge of New South Wales (117), United Grand Lodge of Queensland (120), Grand
Lodge of South Australia and the Northern Territory (123), Grand Lodge of Tasmania
(124), United Grand Lodge of Victoria (125), Grand Lodge of Western Australia (128);
AUSTRIA: Grand Lodge of Austria (129).
BELGIUM: Regular Grand Lodge of Belgium (130); BENIN: Grand Lodge of Benin
(130); BOLIVIA: Grand Lodge of Bolivia (131); BOSNIA AND HERZEGOVINA:
Grand Lodge of Bosnia and Herzegovina*; BRAZIL: Grand Orient of Brazil including
its several State Grand Orients (132-152); the following State Grand Lodges - Alagoas
(154), Amapá (154), Amazonas (155), Bahia (155), Federal District (157), Ceará (157),

(173), Rondônia (174), Roraima (175), Santa Catarina (176), São Paulo (177), Sergipe

FASO: Grand Lodge of Burkina Faso (183).

PRINCE HALL GRAND LODGES
The Grand Lodge of California has mutual recognition with a number of Prince Hall Grand
Lodges in the United States. In addition, the Grand Lodge of California has extended
an offer of recognition to a number of Prince Hall Grand Lodges throughout the country
where mutual recognition does not yet exist.
The Grand Lodge of California maintains mutual recognition with following Prince Hall
Grand Lodges. Some of these Grand Lodges are published in the List of Lodges Masonic
so the same markings follow the name of the Grand Lodge to indicate whether or not the
Grand Lodge is published in the List of Lodges Masonic, and if so, the 2009 edition page
number is provided. The terms of recognition are the same with each Prince Hall Grand
Lodge and are consistent with the agreement of mutual recognition between the Grand
Lodge of California and the Prince Hall Grand Lodge of California, Inc. In essence, dual
membership in Lodges under the two jurisdictions is not allowed. Visitation may be had
between members of Lodges of both jurisdictions. For these purposes, visitation means
visiting tiled meetings.

CALIFORNIA: Prince Hall Grand Lodge of California, Inc. (26); DISTRICT
OF COLUMBIA: Prince Hall Grand Lodge of the District of Columbia*
(includes Lodges in Maryland); MASSACHUSETTS: Prince Hall Grand
Lodge Jurisdiction of Massachusetts* (includes Lodges in New Hampshire and
Maine); MISSOURI: Prince Hall Grand Lodge of Missouri and Jurisdiction*;
NEBRASKA: Prince Hall Grand Lodge of Nebraska and Its Jurisdiction*;
NEW MEXICO: Prince Hall Grand Lodge of the State of New Mexico, Inc.*;
OREGON: Prince Hall Grand Lodge of Oregon*; PENNSYLVANIA: Prince
Hall Grand Lodge Jurisdiction of Pennsylvania* (includes Lodges in Ohio);
TEXAS: Prince Hall Grand Lodge Jurisdiction of Texas*.
The Grand Lodge of California has extended an offer of mutual recognition with
the following Prince Hall Grand Lodges, but mutual recognition has not yet been
established. Visitation between our members and members of Lodges under the
jurisdiction of these Grand Lodges can not yet be made. When mutual recognition
is established, the Grand Secretary will notify our Lodges in writing.
ALASKA: Prince Hall Grand Lodge of Alaska and Its Jurisdiction, Inc.*;
ARIZONA: Prince Hall Grand Lodge of Arizona and Its Jurisdiction*;
